Q. 
What is the purpose of the Panel’s testimony?   
1 
A. 
The Panel’s testimony addresses several topics.  First, the Panel will 
2 
describe the Companies’ efforts and proposals to address customer 
3 
affordability – particularly for LMI customers – and to support 
4 
Disadvantaged Communities.1  This testimony will focus on the 
5 
Companies’ efforts and programs to assist LMI customers in paying their 
6 
energy bills and obtaining the benefits of increased energy efficiency 
7 
directed at achieving New York’s clean energy goals, as set forth in the 
8 
Climate Leadership and Community Protection Act (“CLCPA”).   
9 
 
10 
Second, the Panel will discuss the Companies’ customer service quality 
11 
programs and metrics and discuss several proposed programs aimed at 
12 
improving the customer experience and maintaining high rates of overall 
13 
customer satisfaction. 
14 
 
15 
Third, the Panel will discuss the Companies’ proposals to recover 
16 
uncollectible expense and collection replevin costs, as well as the 
17 
1 The CLCPA established a Climate Justice Working Group (“CJWG”) charged with 
developing and identifying criteria “to ensure frontline and underserved communities’ 
benefit” from the clean energy transition.  On March 27, 2023, the CJWG formally adopted 
the criteria and maps for defining and identifying Disadvantaged Communities.  The 
Companies use the CJWG’s definition when referring to Disadvantaged Communities in 
their testimony. See https://climate.ny.gov/resources/disadvantaged-communities-criteria/. 
Page 8 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 11 ---
Companies’ proposal to adopt a “no-fee” model for accepting residential 
1 
customer payments using debit or credit cards. 
2 
 
3 
Fourth, the Panel will discuss the status and benefits of National Grid’s 
4 
consolidation of its operating companies’ two customer information 
5 
systems into a single CSS system. 
6 
 
7 
Fifth, the Panel will discuss the Companies’ Energy Efficiency and Demand 
8 
Response programs, their role in achieving the goals of the CLCPA, various 
9 
proposed enhancements to the programs and the recovery of associated 
10 
costs. 
11 
 
12 
Finally, the Panel will discuss the Companies’ proposed economic 
13 
development programs, including enhancements to support the goals of the 
14 
CLCPA. 
 
15

10 
II. 
Affordability 
11 
Q. 
Please provide a brief overview of the demographics of the Companies’ 
12 
customer base.   
13 
A. 
National Grid serves approximately 1.3 million customers in New York 
14 
City, including Brooklyn, Queens, and Staten Island through KEDNY and 
15 
600,000 customers in Nassau and Suffolk Counties on Long Island and the 
16 
Rockaway Peninsula in Queens through KEDLI.  Based on National Grid’s 
17 
calculations using demographic data matched against the Companies’ 
18 
residential customer data, and applying the New York State Energy 
19 
Research 
and 
Development 
Authority’s 
(“NYSERDA”) 
income 
20 
Page 11 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 14 ---
guidelines,2 National Grid determined that 32 percent of the Companies’ 
1 
New York City residential customers and 17 percent of the Companies’ 
2 
Long Island residential customers qualify as low-income with annual 
3 
incomes less than 60 percent of the state median income.  When adding 
4 
moderate-income customers, those at 60 to 80 percent of the area median 
5 
income for New York City and Long Island, the percentage of low- and 
6 
moderate-income (“LMI”) residential customers in the Companies’ service 
7 
territories rises to nearly 47 percent in New York City and 29 percent on 
8 
Long Island.  
9 
 
10 
Q.  
What is the significance of this data?  
11 
A. 
This data highlights the fact that affordability is a key concern for our 
12 
customers, especially those with low- and moderate-incomes.  The 
13 
Commission has affirmed the importance of maintaining affordability for 
14 
customers, highlighting the need to identify cost-effective investments as 
15 
part of the collective effort to achieve the CLCPA goals.  Programs that 
16 
assist customers with managing their energy usage and paying their bills are 
17 
essential to help customers meet their energy needs.  
18 
 
19 
2 The NYSERDA income guidelines can be found at:  https://www.nyserda.ny.gov/All-
Programs/assisted-home-performance-with-energy-star/income-guidelines. 
Page 12 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 15 ---
Q. 
Have the Companies conducted any additional research concerning 
1 
LMI customers?   
2 
A. 
Yes.  National Grid conducted both qualitative and quantitative research 
3 
into LMI customer needs.  In early 2022, as further detailed below, National 
4 
Grid engaged ESource, a research, consulting, and data science firm, to 
5 
conduct a study to help the Companies develop a better understanding of 
6 
their LMI customers’ energy needs and to create a forward-thinking 
7 
customer experience strategy.  The study confirmed that LMI customers’ 
8 
biggest concern is bill affordability.  They want to pay their utility bills but 
9 
worry about their ability to pay.  Customers want more payment options and 
10 
flexibility and for the Companies to provide them with programs that are 
11 
easy to navigate.  They are also interested in making energy efficiency 
12 
improvements.   
13 
 
14 
Q. 
How have the Companies used the findings from National Gird’s 
15 
research and the CLCPA’s focus on delivering benefits to 
16 
Disadvantaged Communities to develop the customer service and 
17 
energy efficiency proposals in these rate filings?   
18 
A. 
The Companies have taken the research findings and customer feedback to 
19 
heart and are proposing a number of customer service and energy efficiency 
20 
programs and initiatives designed to address the feedback and deliver 
21 
Page 13 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 16 ---
customer benefits, while managing costs.  The Companies are also 
1 
proposing initiatives to improve customer service efficiency and 
2 
productivity.   
3 
 
4 
Q. 
How is National Grid working to support the State’s CLCPA 
5 
requirement that Disadvantaged Communities receive at least 35 
6 
percent of overall benefits of spending on clean energy and energy 
7 
efficiency programs, projects, or investments? 
8 
A. 
National Grid believes energy efficiency is a key driver of an equitable 
9 
energy transition for customers.  National Grid began an internal project in 
10 
2022 to identify and map Disadvantaged Communities within the operating 
11 
companies’ service territories,3 and to develop energy efficiency products 
12 
and programs to meet customer needs in the communities.  Increasing 
13 
participation in these programs in Disadvantaged Communities requires a 
14 
concerted effort to address significant barriers to access, including 
15 
economic challenges and the lack of language accessibility.  With this in 
16 
mind, the Companies are advancing a number of proposals intended to 
17 
overcome these hurdles and enable customers in Disadvantaged 
18 
Communities to realize the benefits of energy efficiency offerings, as 
19 
3 On December 13, 2021, the CJWG, as part of the draft criteria, issued an interactive 
map identifying Disadvantaged Communities. 
Page 14 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 17 ---
discussed in more detail below.  The Companies anticipate that, as part of 
1 
the NE:NY Interim Review in Case 18-M-0084, the Commission may 
2 
identify additional energy efficiency programs to best meet the needs of the 
3 
State’s Disadvantaged Communities and drive further action toward 
4 
achieving the CLCPA goals.  
5 
   
6 
Q. 
Please identify the Companies’ current programs, products and 
7 
services that assist LMI customers. 
8 
A. 
KEDNY and KEDLI have a long-standing commitment to LMI programs 
9 
and continue to work proactively to support their most vulnerable 
10 
customers.  The diverse demographics, energy usage profiles, housing, and 
11 
costs of living across the Companies’ service territories can impact energy 
12 
affordability.  Energy costs place a relatively larger burden on LMI families, 
13 
as energy bills represent a larger percentage of income than for other 
14 
households.  To assist these customers while managing affordability for all 
15 
customers, the Companies are committed to balancing LMI benefits and 
16 
program costs.   
17 
 
18 
The Companies offer a low-income bill discount program, referred to as the 
19 
Energy Affordability Program (“EAP”), that provides a monthly credit on 
20 
enrolled customers’ bills (discussed in further detail below).  The 
21 
Page 15 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 18 ---
Companies also offer several bill-assistance programs, such as budget 
1 
billing, deferred payment agreements (“DPAs”), and specialized payment 
2 
arrangements. In addition, KEDLI supports the United Way of Long 
3 
Island’s Project Warmth.  Likewise, KEDNY’s similar Neighborhood 
4 
Heating Fund program is administered by HeartShare Human Services.  In 
5 
2022, the Companies partnered with HeartShare Human Services to donate 
6 
additional money to the fuel fund and start two new programs to support 
7 
their customers, the Hope and Warmth Energy Fund and Hearts Fighting 
8 
Hunger.  The Hope and Warmth Energy Fund provides moderate-income 
9 
customers who qualify with a $200 grant during the winter heating season.  
10 
The Hearts Fighting Hunger program provides moderate-income customers 
11 
with a vulnerable household member who qualify a $150 grocery store gift 
12 
card/food voucher.   
13 
 
14 
To further assist customers with their energy bills, the Companies have a 
15 
Consumer Advocacy program where customers can meet directly with an 
16 
advocate who will help them navigate their energy bills and available 
17 
assistance programs.  As discussed below, the Companies also offer a suite 
18 
of LMI energy efficiency programs as part of the NE:NY Statewide LMI 
19 
Energy Efficiency Portfolio that are aimed at helping manage customers’ 
20 
Page 16 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 19 ---
energy usage through weatherization and heating system upgrades, which 
1 
also may improve customers’ health, safety and comfort.  
2 
 
3 
A. Low-Income Bill Discount Program – Additional EAP Resources 
4 
Q. 
Please describe the EAP and any other programs offered by the 
5 
Companies to assist low-income customers.  
6 
A.  
KEDNY and KEDLI’s EAP provides low-income customers with a 
7 
monthly fixed credit on their bill.  The amount of the credit is based on the 
8 
customer’s Home Energy Assistance Program (“HEAP”) benefit and offers 
9 
a four-tier structure.  Consideration is given to the customers’ income, 
10 
energy costs, and family size.  If applicable, there are two add-ons available 
11 
to eligible households as part of the regular HEAP program to assist the 
12 
neediest households — lowest income and vulnerable household member.  
13 
Households receiving a regular HEAP benefit are enrolled in Tier 1.  
14 
Households receiving one HEAP add-on are enrolled in Tier 2 and 
15 
households with two HEAP add-ons are enrolled in Tier 3.  Households 
16 
enrolled in Tier 4 are currently enrolled in a Utility Guarantee/Direct 
17 
Voucher (“GU/DV”) program.  The tiered benefit level chart is set forth 
18 
below.   
19 
 
20 
Page 17 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 20 ---
KEDNY also has a Reconnect Fee Waiver program for customers 
1 
participating in the EAP who have had their service disconnected for non-
2 
payment.  KEDLI does not charge low-income customers reconnect fees.  
3 
No change is proposed to the Reconnect Fee Waiver program. 
4 
 
5 
Q. 
What are the benefit amounts for each tier based on the Commission’s 
6 
Low-Income Order (Case 14-M-0565)?  
7

8 
are as follows:

Q.  
Who is eligible to participate in the Companies’ EAPs and how are 
8 
customers enrolled? 
9 
A.  
KEDNY and KEDLI customers who have received a HEAP grant or are 
10 
enrolled in a GU/DV program are automatically enrolled in the EAP when

13

14

15

17

18 
KEDLI’s program had an average of 13,064 participants and an annual rate 
19

20

Testimony of The Customer Panel

million, an overspend of $2.17 million, which was also due to increasing

3 
Q. 
What are the proposed annual rate allowances for the EAP in the Rate 
4 
Years and the Data Years?  
5 
A.  
Proposed rate allowances for KEDNY and KEDLI’s EAP use forecast 
6 
program budgets, which are based on anticipated participation and tier 
7 
discount amounts calculated in accordance with the Commission’s orders 
8 
in Case 14-M-0565.  The forecast annual rate allowance included in the 
9 
revenue requirement for KEDNY and KEDLI in the Rate Year and Data 
10 
Years is $46.90 million and $8.85 million, respectively, an increase of $8.45 
11 
million and $2.10 million, respectively from the current rate allowance.  
12 
Pursuant to the Commission’s orders, the Companies are required to adjust 
13 
benefit levels to keep program costs within the two percent budget cap. The 
14 
testimony of the Rate Design Panels discusses the calculation of 
15 
incremental EAP costs associated with the proposed rate increases and the 
16 
issues that arise as a result of the updated discount calculations.

Q. 
Do the Companies currently reconcile EAP costs?  
1 
A. 
Yes.  In accordance with the Commission’s order in the 2019 KEDNY and 
2 
KEDLI Rate Cases, the Companies fully reconcile the amount recovered in 
3 
the rates to the actual cost of the EAP each year and defer any differences.  
4 
 
5 
Q. 
How do the Companies propose to recover costs of the EAP? 
6 
A. 
The Companies propose to continue recovering the costs associated with 
7 
the EAP through base rates.   
8 
 
9 
Q. 
Do the Companies propose any changes to the current reconciliation 
10 
mechanism? 
11 
A. 
Yes.  Under the current reconciliation mechanism, if actual annual program 
12 
costs are below the amount recovered in rates, the Companies would 
13 
establish a deferred credit for use in future low-income programs and if the 
14 
actual annual program costs exceed the amount recovered in rates, the 
15 
Companies would establish a deferral for future recovery from all 
16 
customers.  As described in the testimony of the Rate Design Panels, the 
17 
Companies are proposing to include any over or under recoveries in the 
18 
proposed Rate Adjustment Mechanism (“RAM”).

B. Consumer Advocacy Program 
4 
Q. 
Please summarize the Companies’ Consumer Advocacy programs. 
5 
A. 
KEDNY and KEDLI have a longstanding commitment to their Consumer 
6 
Advocacy programs. Consumer Advocates support the Companies’ most 
7 
vulnerable customers by connecting them with programs and services 
8 
offered by the Companies and service organizations within their 
9 
communities.  They assist customers who have special needs (e.g., elderly, 
10 
blind, and disabled, or serious medical conditions), special circumstances 
11 
(e.g., domestic violence, death of a family member, recent crime victim, 
12 
victim of sex trafficking, customers affected by damaging storms and 
13 
floods, those who are new to the U.S.), or those experiencing financial 
14 
hardship.  In calendar year 2022, Consumer Advocates assisted 11,895 
15 
households and participated in 189 outreach and education events in the 
16 
KEDNY and KEDLI service territories. 
17 
 
18 
Q. 
Please describe the services the Consumer Advocates provide. 
19 
A. 
The Consumer Advocates are embedded in their communities and are 
20 
constantly looking for opportunities to identify low-income and vulnerable 
21 
Page 27 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 30 ---
customers through referrals made by the contact centers, field personnel, 
1 
agencies, and through customer self-referral.  In addition, they make 
2 
weekly, bi-monthly, and monthly visits to selected agencies such as the 
3 
Department of Social Services, Community Action Program, Economic 
4 
Opportunity Council offices, National Grid walk-in offices, Long Island 
5 
Cares, Adelante of Suffolk County, the Salvation Army, the Huntington 
6 
Resource Center, the Family Service League, one-stop job training centers, 
7 
food pantries, domestic violence organizations, health clinics, public 
8 
libraries, and senior apartment complexes.   
9 
 
10 
These efforts focus on bringing awareness to available programs and 
11 
services, such as the EAP, specialized payment arrangements, DPAs, bill 
12 
extender programs, budget plan/balanced billing, online bill payment,  
13 
KEDLI’s HEAT program, and the Companies’ support fuel funds (i.e., 
14 
Neighborhood Heating Fund and Project Warmth and Hearts Fighting 
15 
Hunger and Hope and Warmth).  Consumer Advocates are also trained to 
16 
help customers access other local, State, or federal programs such as 
17 
Temporary Assistance, HEAP, the Weatherization Assistance Program, and 
18 
EmPower NY.

11

12

13 
LMI customers who could benefit from these programs.  
14 
 
15 
The need for additional Consumer Advocates is being driven by the growth 
16 
in the number of customers who can benefit from access to the myriad 
17 
programs available to assist them.

C. Collection Residential Account Management Group 
4 
Q. 
Please describe the role of the employees of the collection residential 
5 
account management (“CRAM”) group. 
6 
A. 
The CRAM group specializes in direct engagement via phone call and email 
7 
with high-balance residential accounts that require extra assistance in 
8 
reducing their balances.  The group focuses on building relationships with 
9 
the customers to provide financial options and assistance, improve the 
10 
customer experience, and help to avoid service terminations. 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Please describe how the Companies’ assist low-income customers 
13 
through the work of the CRAM group.  
14 
A. 
The CRAM group helps low-income customers by providing assistance in 
15 
setting up different types of DPAs.  The group also provides guidance on 
16 
different assistance programs, such as HEAP, Department of Social 
17 
Services vouchers and guarantees, Neighborhood Heating Fund, Project 
18 
Warmth, Catholic Charities, and Salvation Army. 
19 
 
20 
Page 36 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 39 ---
Q. 
What type of customers most benefit from the CRAM group? 
1 
A. 
Low-income, high balance, customers receive the most benefit from the 
2 
CRAM group via the direct interaction and subsequent guidance on DPAs 
3 
and assistance programs that may be available.  The CRAM group helps 
4 
these customers avoid service termination by working directly with them to 
5 
reduce their past-due balance. 
6 
 
7 
Q. 
How does the CRAM group differ from the Consumer Advocates? 
8 
A. 
Consumer Advocates primary role is providing intervention with a focus on 
9 
LMI customers.  The CRAM group specializes in direct engagement 
10 
through phone calls and emails with high-balance residential customers that 
11 
require additional assistance in reducing their account balances and 
12 
preventing service terminations.  The CRAM group provides a single point 
13 
of contact and builds long-term relationships with customers to provide 
14 
financial options and identify assistance programs that can help these 
15 
customers.  The CRAM group improves the experience of high-balance, 
16 
low-income customers and helps them to avoid service terminations.   
17 
 
18

Testimony of The Customer Panel

17

18

19 
A. 
The centerpieces of the Companies’ customer service efforts are the 
20

21

Testimony of The Customer Panel

Place, Brooklyn, New York.  The KEDNY and KEDLI call centers are full-

8 
office, the Companies established a sustainable, work-from-home 
9 
environment for the Customer Service staff.  As a result, call center 
10 
employees enjoy the flexibility to work from home, rotating into the Hanson 
11 
Place office for training, coaching, and team meetings.  The call centers also 
12 
utilize New York State-based third-party vendors to handle some moves and 
13 
collections calls. 
14 
 
15 
The Companies also have a group dedicated to handling escalated 
16 
complaints, the Office of the President.  The Office of the President team 
17 
consists of 14 full-time employees dedicated to resolving customer issues 
18 
forwarded from the Commission consistent with the Commission’s Quick 
19 
Resolution System (“QRS”) process, ensuring that the Companies’ policies 
20 
and Commission’s regulations are followed consistently. 
21 
Page 39 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 42 ---
Under the QRS process, customer complaints received by the Commission 
1 
are sent to the affected company for resolution.  If the affected company 
2 
and the customer cannot resolve the issue and it is sent back to the 
3 
Commission by the customer, the company, or both, the complaint is 
4 
categorized as part of the Commission’s Standard Resolution System 
5 
(“SRS”) process.  Complaints reaching the SRS level are considered 
6 
charged complaints for purposes of the Companies’ complaint rate 
7 
performance metric. 
8 
 
9

12 
KEDLI are: (i) annual Commission complaint rate per 100,000 customers, 
13 
(ii) residential customer transaction satisfaction, (iii) percentage of 
14 
customer calls answered in 30 seconds, and (iv) adjusted customer bills.

Q. 
Are the Companies proposing any changes to their service quality 
1

2

3

4

5

6 
 
7 
Q. 
Why are the Companies proposing to continue the current customer 
8 
service quality metrics other than the customer satisfaction survey? 
9 
A. 
Based on an assessment of five years of performance data, the Companies 
10 
believe that the current targets are appropriate.  Data shows that the existing 
11 
targets are difficult to achieve and sufficiently challenging to ensure an 
12 
appropriate level of service, especially during periods of peak demand or 
13 
extreme temperatures.  Moreover, the results of the Residential Transaction 
14 
Satisfaction Index show consistently high levels of overall customer 
15 
satisfaction.  Thus, there is no basis to conclude that more stringent targets 
16 
are needed to encourage the Companies to be more responsive to customers. 
17 
 
18 
Page 42 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 45 ---
Q. 
Are the Companies proposing to align the KEDNY and KEDLI 
1 
Customer Satisfaction surveys with the Joint Utility Statewide Survey 
2 
(“Joint Utility Survey”)? 
3 
A. 
Yes, the Companies are proposing this change; however, the historical 
4 
survey data is not sufficient to set a target.  Although the Companies have 
5 
been collecting KEDLI data since September 2018 and KEDNY data since 
6 
January 2019, much of this time included the COVID-19 pandemic and 
7 
associated collections moratorium (i.e., March 2020 to September 2021 for 
8 
KEDLI and March 2020 to November 2021 for KEDNY).  After removing 
9 
the months that were impacted by the moratorium, the Companies are left 
10 
with 25 months of data for KEDNY and 33 months of data for KEDLI, half 
11 
of which was collected before March 2020.  Before adopting the Joint 
12 
Utility Survey and establishing targets, the Companies would like to collect 
13 
more post-moratorium data to ensure target accuracy.  
14 
 
15 
Q. 
Why is the available data insufficient?  
16 
A.  
COVID-19 resulted in many special programs for customers, including the 
17 
suspension of collections and debt forgiveness.  For that reason, the results 
18 
appear inflated compared to the limited data that was collected prior to 
19 
COVID, which impacts the ability to set an appropriate target.  The 
20 
Companies have only recently returned to pre-COVID procedures. 
21

A. Permitting Third-Party Vendors To Use Call Center Agents Who 
1 
Live Outside New York 
2 
Q. 
Are the Companies proposing to permit their third-party call center 
3 
vendors to use call center agents who live outside New York? 
4 
A. 
Yes.  The Companies propose to improve the quality and efficiency of call 
5 
center operations by enabling third-party vendors to utilize home-based 
6 
agents who live outside the State of New York.  
7 
 
8 
Q. 
Do the Companies currently use third-party vendors to support 
9 
limited aspects of call center operations? 
10 
A. 
Yes.  The Companies currently use third-party vendors that use New York-
11 
based agents to handle certain types of customer calls. 
12 
 
13 
Q. 
What type of customer calls do the third-party vendors handle for the 
14 
Companies? 
15 
A. 
Third-party vendors assist with calls made by customers when they move 
16 
within the service territory (“Move Calls”) and calls concerning customer 
17 
collection activities.  Move Calls consist of a new customer calling for 
18 
connecting service at an existing premise or an existing customer, 
19 
disconnecting service or moving to a new location.  Collections calls consist 
20 
Page 47 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 50 ---
of payments, inquiries concerning short- and long-term payment 
1 
agreements, protections, and any other related past-due inquiries. 
2 
  
3 
Q. 
What percentage of various types of customer calls are allocated to 
4 
third-party vendors?  
5 
A. 
Currently, 100 percent of collections and Move Calls by KEDLI customers 
6 
and 100 percent of collections and 70 percent of Move Calls for KEDNY 
7 
customers are allocated to third-party vendors.  Agents who handle both 
8 
Move Calls and collections calls are currently required to reside in New 
9 
York.  It is the Companies’ understanding that a hearing before the 
10 
Commission must take place before the Companies can relocate any call 
11 
center operations out of state.  
12 
 
13 
Q. 
How is the performance of the third-party vendors managed by the 
14 
Companies? 
15 
A. 
The Companies manage third-party vendors in the same manner as internal 
16 
operations.  The Companies have established contractual Key Performance 
17 
Indicators (“KPIs”) that measure performance in areas such as service level, 
18 
average handle time, first contact resolution, and customer satisfaction.  
19 
The Companies hold weekly, monthly and quarterly performance reviews 
20 
Page 48 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 51 ---
with third-party vendors to ensure that their performance meets the 
1 
Companies’ expectations. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Why do the Companies propose to allow their third-party vendors to 
4 
use call center agents that live outside New York? 
5 
A. 
During the COVID-19 pandemic, it became common for call center agents 
6 
to work remotely.  Thus, the Companies, as well as the Companies’ third-
7 
party vendors, are utilizing work-from-home options to attract and retain 
8 
quality employees.  At the same time, recruiting for call center positions in 
9 
New York, specifically downstate New York, has become particularly 
10 
challenging.  While the Companies will continue to attempt to hire call 
11 
center employees who live in New York, it has become increasingly 
12 
difficult to attract and retain New York-based employees due to an increase 
13 
in higher wage opportunities for other types of employment.  Enabling 
14 
third-party vendors to use resources outside New York State will allow 
15 
them to attract candidates from a wider range of employment markets, 
16 
affording them the opportunity to achieve greater efficiencies and reduce 
17 
attrition.  Under the Companies’ proposal, employees of the third-party 
18 
vendors (except for the off-shore vendor discussed below) could be located 
19 
outside of New York but within the continental United States.  Further 
20 
Page 49 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 52 ---
information concerning this proposal is set forth in Exhibit ___ (CP-2), 
1 
Schedule 1.  
2 
 
3 
Q. 
What is the magnitude of the recent turnover for call center 
4 
employees experienced by the Companies?  
5 
A. 
Turnover rates in New York were approximately 10 percent for calendar 
6 
year 2022, an increase of 50 percent compared to pre-COVID rates.  It 
7 
appears that this attrition is attributable to the fact that call enter workers 
8 
are able to find other employment with better pay and/or benefits. 
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Do the Companies believe that reducing attrition among its third-
11 
party call center agents will assist the Companies in providing the 
12 
service that customers expect? 
13 
A. 
Yes, reducing attrition rates will create a more consistent tenured call 
14 
center agent population, which will assist in improved first call resolution, 
15 
better enable the Companies to maintain high levels of customer 
16 
satisfaction, increase employee satisfaction, and permit the Companies to 
17 
avoid increased training costs that arise from high levels of attrition. 
18 
 
19 
Page 50 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 53 ---

1 
reduce attrition rates?

A.

3

4 
that prefer to work a Saturday shift, and offer comparable pay and benefits 
5 
to like job descriptions.   
6

Gas Corporation currently utilize call center staff outside New York State.

B. Enhancing Communications for Spanish-Speaking Customers 
1 
Q. 
Please describe the Companies’ proposed changes to call center 
2 
operations for Spanish-speaking customers. 
3 
A. 
The Companies propose to improve call center operations by enabling a 
4 
third-party vendor to utilize their near-offshore facilities to manage the 
5 
Companies’ inbound Spanish call volume associated with moves, 
6 
customer connections and collections.  No billing or emergency calls 
7 
would be assigned to this vendor.  This proposal involves securing 
8 
resources with one vendor in Guatemala or Jamaica to manage this volume.  
9 
Further information concerning this proposal is set forth on Exhibit ___ 
10 
(CP-2), Schedule 2. 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
What is the current process for managing Spanish-speaking inbound 
13 
calls?  
14 
A. 
When a Spanish-speaking customer contacts the Companies’ inbound Call 
15 
Center, the customer is connected with a Spanish-speaking agent, if 
16 
available.  If not, the customer is routed to an English-speaking agent.  
17 
That agent then connects to the Companies’ language interpreter service, 
18 
which translates the conversation.  In calendar year 2022, the Companies 
19 
utilized a Spanish interpreter for just over 77,000 calls for KEDNY and 
20 
just over 12,000 calls for KEDLI. 
21 
Page 52 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 55 ---
Q. 
What types of challenges does the current process create for 
1 
customers?  
2 
A. 
Customers want to speak to an agent in their native language.  Utilizing 
3 
an interpreter causes added length to a call; confusion in some cases due 
4 
to the back and forth with the customer, National Grid agent, and 
5 
interpreter agent; and overall a less effective customer service customer 
6 
experience. 
7 
 
8 
Q. 
Do any affiliates of the Companies use near-offshore resources for 
9 
inbound Spanish-speaking calls? 
10 
A. 
Yes.  National Grid’s New England third-party vendor utilizes English and 
11 
Spanish-speaking employees who are located in Guatemala.  The Spanish 
12 
dialect in Guatemala is very similar to the Spanish-speaking population’s 
13 
dialect in the Northeast United States, making it transparent and easy to 
14 
understand for customers. 
15 
 
16 
Q. 
What feedback have the Companies’ affiliates received from the 
17 
customers currently being serviced with near-offshore resources in 
18 
New England? 
19 
A. 
The Companies’ affiliates have received positive feedback, as evidenced 
20 
by the following testimonials:

C. Other Customer Experience Programs 
9 
Q. 
What other programs are the Companies proposing to undertake to 
10 
improve the Customer Experience?   
11 
A. 
The Companies are proposing to undertake seven additional programs to 
12 
modernize and enhance the customer experience.  These programs include: 
13 
(1) optimizing the front-office delivery model (the manner in which calls 
14 
are handled) for the Companies’ contact center, (2) reducing calls with self-
15 
service, (3) minimizing average call handling time, (4) first call resolution, 
16 
(5) automating manual work, (6) optimizing back-office processes and 
17 
capacity, and (7) promoting e-bill (Paperless) adoption.  Additional 
18 
information concerning these efforts is set forth in Exhibit ___ (CP-2), 
19 
Schedule 3.  The Companies’ proposed capital expenditures associated with 
20 
these programs can be found in the IT Panel’s Exhibit ___ (ITDP-3).  
21 
Page 55 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 58 ---
Q. 
What is the primary driver for these programs? 
1 
A. 
The primary driver of these programs is to improve the reliability of critical 
2 
systems and enhance existing business processes to maintain or improve 
3 
existing levels of customer satisfaction in a changing business environment.  
4 
Dependence on traditional customer interactions via telephone and mail is 
5 
inefficient compared to the digitally focused customer experience that the 
6 
Companies are proposing.  The Companies currently utilize technology that 
7 
relies on manual processes.  The proposed programs will enable 
8 
technology-based solutions that customers are increasingly seeking. 
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Will these programs create savings? 
11 
A. 
Yes.  These savings are captured as part of the Evolution program savings 
12 
that are reflected in the Companies’ revenue requirements and discussed by 
13 
the Companies’ Revenue Requirements Panels. 
14 
 
15 
Q. 
Please describe how optimizing the front office delivery model for the 
16 
Companies’ contact center will enable the Companies to maintain a 
17 
high level of customer service. 
18 
A. 
Optimizing Call Center operations through management of internal and 
19 
vendor workforce planning will permit the Companies to better respond to 
20 
customer calls.  The program seeks to enhance customer satisfaction by: (1) 
21 
Page 56 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 59 ---
improving the customer experiences on the Unified Web Portal (“UWP”) 
1 
to enable customers to better manage billing accounts with single email sign 
2 
on, (2) proactively providing the right information through the customer’s 
3 
preferred communication channel (e.g., via email or text), (3) providing 
4 
customers with simpler, effortless and personalized experiences through 
5 
improved voice self-service (i.e., interactive voice response (“IVR”)), (4) 
6 
delivering customers opportunities for improved and new experiences on 
7 
digital channels that utilize virtual assistants (digital assistants), and (5) 
8 
delivering an enhanced digital platform (i.e., My Business Account) to 
9 
commercial and industrial customers to enable better and more streamlined 
10 
multi-account management.   
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Please describe how the Companies will minimize average call handle 
13 
times. 
14 
A. 
This program focuses on process enhancements and performance 
15 
management to drive efficient and effective calls with customers.  By 
16 
optimizing the process by which customers who contact the Companies are 
17 
greeted and authenticated, customers will not have to provide duplicate 
18 
information.  This will result in both enhanced customer service and more 
19 
efficient communication with customers. 
20 
 
21 
Page 57 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 60 ---
Q. 
Please describe the First Call Resolution program. 
1 
A. 
The First Call Resolution program focuses on the Customer Service 
2 
Representative (“CSR”) successfully addressing an inquiry during the 
3 
customer’s first contact with the call center, eliminating the need for call 
4 
backs with the same issues.  Areas of opportunity include customer moves 
5 
and billing interactions that make up 65.8 percent of repeat calls today.  This 
6 
program will also enable the Companies to reduce call transfer rates and 
7 
keep calls contained with one CSR.  This will improve customer trust, avoid 
8 
unnecessary time spent on calls by the customers and improve overall 
9 
customer satisfaction. 
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Please describe the benefits of the program that will automate manual 
12 
work. 
13 
A. 
In this program, the Companies will deploy Robotic Process Automation to 
14 
reduce manual efforts.  This will create operational efficiencies by reducing 
15 
volumes that require manual processing and automating processing, which 
16 
will, in turn, increase standardization and create capacity for the 
17 
Companies’ call center agents to help customers with more complex or 
18 
value-added tasks.  
19 
 
20 
Page 58 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 61 ---
Q. 
Please describe the Companies’ programs for optimizing back-office 
1 
processes and capacity. 
2 
A. 
The Billing and Collections office (“Back Office”) will use a centralized 
3 
platform to optimize workforce performance with the ability to forecast, 
4 
plan and schedule more effectively.  The Companies will be able to more 
5 
accurately measure employee performance (such as the time when 
6 
employees are unproductive and work handle time) and improve 
7 
productivity.   
8 
 
9 
Q. 
Please describe how e-bill (paperless) adoption will enhance the 
10 
customer experience. 
11 
A. 
Encouraging customer participation in the paperless program will result in 
12 
an easier and more convenient billing experience.  The Companies will also 
13 
measure and remediate the key reasons why customers return to paper 
14 
billing and redesign email notifications to increase efficacy and ease of use.  
15 
Customer analytics will target customer segments with tailored marketing 
16 
campaigns that demonstrate the benefits of e-billing to encourage greater e-
17 
billing usage.

Q. 
How will the proposed customer experience programs improve service 
1 
to the Companies’ customers? 
2 
A. 
Creating digital capabilities will allow customers to resolve inquiries on a 
3 
self-service basis in the manner they prefer (chat bot, web, IVR) and 
4 
decrease time spent on the phone.  Process automation will focus on 
5 
decreasing manual, repetitive tasks for the Companies’ CSRs which will 
6 
increase the ability of the representatives to focus on more complex 
7 
customer issues, resulting in improved customer experience and 
8 
satisfaction.  As a result of the implementation of the proposed customer 
9 
experience programs, customer agents will educate customers on the ease 
10 
of accessing their billing information and paying their bills electronically.  
11 
Finally, by delivering outbound communications via email and text 
12 
messaging, customers will have correct information in real-time, including 
13 
information about their bills or communications about outages or upcoming 
14 
storms. 
15 
 
16 
Q. 
What benefits do the programs to improve the customer experience 
17 
have on the environment and GHG emissions?  
18 
A. 
Electronic billing is expected to reduce GHG emissions by decreasing 
19 
reliance on paper, postage, and emissions related to the delivery of such 
20 
paper notifications to customers.

V. 
Uncollectible Expense 
1 
Q. 
Please provide an overview of the impact from the Companies pausing 
2 
collection activity during the COVID-19 pandemic. 
3 
A. 
The Companies voluntarily suspended collection activity for all customers 
4 
in March 2020 due to the impacts from COVID.  Customer protections were 
5 
extended to customers through the Parker-Mosley Act and the Parker-
6 
Richardson Act (the “Acts”).  During this period, residential and 
7 
commercial customers had the option to attest to financial hardship and 
8 
access 
various 
customer 
protections 
including 
protection 
from 
9 
disconnection for non-payment.  
10 
  
11 
Commercial collection activity slowly ramped up in 2021 while residential 
12 
field disconnections slowly resumed in June 2022 at KEDNY and July 2022 
13 
at KEDLI.  During the disconnection moratorium both residential and 
14 
commercial customer built up large arrears balances.  As of June 2022, the 
15 
number of customers in 60-day arrears increased by 27 percent at KEDNY 
16 
and 15 percent at KEDLI compared to June 2019 and accounts receivable 
17 
balances increased by 191 percent at KEDNY and 132 percent at KEDLI.   
18 
  
19 
In June 2022, the Commission authorized Phase 1 of the New York Electric 
20 
and Gas Arrears Relief program (Cases 14-M-0565 and 20-M-0266).  This 
21 
Page 61 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 64 ---
program provided arrears relief to low-income customers and customers 
1 
who received Emergency Rental Assistance Program (“ERAP”) assistance 
2 
through a one-time credit of all arrears outstanding that were billed through 
3 
May 1, 2022. The Companies applied the first set of credits in July 2022. 
4 
Customers enrolling in a low-income program (i.e., EAP) or receiving 
5 
ERAP assistance through December 31, 2022 also received the credit. In 
6 
total, approximately 45,300 KEDNY customers received credits of $50.1 
7 
million, while 4,200 KEDLI customers received $5.3 million of credits.   
8 
  
9 
In January 2023, the Commission authorized Phase 2 of the New York 
10 
Electric and Gas Arrears Relief program.  This phase of the program 
11 
provided arrears relief to the residential customers who did not receive a 
12 
credit during Phase 1 as well as to small commercial customers.  These 
13 
customers received an “up-to” credit for arrears outstanding that were billed 
14 
through May 1, 2022.  KEDNY residential customers were eligible for up 
15 
to a $3,750 credit while small commercial customers were eligible for up to 
16 
a $7,500 credit.  KEDLI residential customers were eligible for up to a 
17 
$1,750 credit while small commercial customers were eligible for up to a 
18 
$2,000 credit. Credits were applied on February 22, 2023 with 
19 
approximately 62,300 KEDNY customers receiving credits of $82.2 million 
20 
and 21,900 KEDLI customers receiving credits of $16.9 million.   
21 
Page 62 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 65 ---
  
The New York State HEAP Regular Arrears Supplement (“RAS”) program 
1 
has also had a positive impact on KEDNY and KEDLI residential 
2 
customers.  In late September 2021, New York rolled out the $150 million 
3 
RAS program to assist customers who received heating assistance during 
4 
the year.  In addition, federal funding of $2.2 billion was directed to New 
5

6 
rental and utility assistance.

After assisting customers through the Phase 1 and Phase 2 programs, there 
9

10

11 
levels.  Although the Phase 1 and Phase 2 programs were very helpful for 
12 
customers in need, as of February 2023 (after Phase 2 credits were applied), 
13

14 
(approximately 32 percent at KEDNY and 30 percent at KEDLI) compared 
15 
to February 2020 and accounts receivable balances increased by 74 percent 
16 
at KEDNY and 61 percent at KEDLI.  The Companies anticipate that this 
17 
will result in a backlog of accounts requiring additional collection activities 
18 
as well as increased uncollectible bill costs.  To meet the needs to manage 
19 
the increased collection activity, coupled with the fact that collection 
20 
activity was paused during part of the Historic Test Year, the Companies 
21

Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 66 ---
are proposing to use a three-year pre-COVID average of replevin costs 
1 
incurred during CY 2017, 2018 and 2019 to establish rates in these 
2 
proceedings.  In addition, the Companies are proposing to use a three-year 
3 
pre-COVID average of uncollectible rates and an uncollectible 
4 
reconciliation mechanism.  These proposals are discussed below.

6 
Q. 
What is the Companies’ recent experience with uncollectible expense? 
7 
A. 
During the COVID-19 pandemic, the Acts required a moratorium on 
8 
collections and termination activity for residential customers.4  Due in part 
9 
to the Acts, the Companies’ uncollectible expenses have been volatile over 
10 
the last three years.  KEDNY’s uncollectible expense increased from $14.6 
11 
million in 2017 to $16.3 million in 2018, then decreased in 2019 to $15.9 
12 
million.  During the COVID-19 pandemic when collection activity was 
13 
paused, uncollectible expense dropped to $7.8 million in 2020, followed by 
14 
an increase in 2021 to $11.5 million and then a further increase in the 
15 
Historic Test Year to $26.5 million, as shown in Exhibit ___ (CP-3), 
16 
Schedule 1, after most collection activity resumed.  KEDLI’s uncollectible 
17 
expense decreased from $7.3 million in 2017 to $7.0 million in 2018, then 
18 
4 Chapter 108 of the Laws of 2020 (the “Parker Mosley Law”) and Chapter 109 of the Laws 
of 2021 (the “Parker-Richardson Act”), which were deemed expired and repealed on July 
1, 2022. 
Page 64 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 67 ---
increased to $7.2 million in 2019.  During the COVID-19 pandemic when 
1 
collection activity was paused, uncollectible expense dropped to $4.1 
2 
million in 2020, rose to $4.5 million in 2021 and then increased in the 
3 
Historic Test Year to $16.8 million, as shown in Exhibit ___ (CP-4), 
4 
Schedule 1. 
5 
 
6 
As set forth below, the short-term effect of reduced collection activity 
7 
during the COVID-19 pandemic is lower uncollectible expense.  However, 
8 
the long-term effect of reducing terminations is to increase accounts 
9 
receivable, which with all else being equal, lead to higher uncollectible 
10 
write-offs when the accounts with larger receivable balances are eventually 
11 
written off. 
12 
 
13 
Q. 
Please describe how uncollectible expense is currently recovered by 
14 
KEDNY and KEDLI. 
15 
A. 
Commodity-related uncollectible expense is recovered through the 
16 
Merchant Function Charge (“MFC”) component of the Gas Adjustment 
17 
Clause (“GAC”) and determined by multiplying the forecast total gas cost 
18 
(inclusive of any monthly or annual imbalance surcharge or refund factors) 
19 
for each GAC year times a net write-off of 0.8864 percent for KEDNY and 
20 
0.6068 percent for KEDLI.  Additionally, Energy Service Companies 
21 
Page 65 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 68 ---
(“ESCOs”) that participate in the Companies’ Purchase of Receivables 
1 
(“POR”) program are charged commodity related uncollectible costs.  
2 
Delivery related uncollectible expense for KEDNY and KEDLI is 
3 
recovered through base delivery rates and is not adjusted annually.   
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Are the Companies proposing to change the calculation of commodity-
6 
related uncollectible expense for firm sales customers? 
7 
A. 
No.  The Companies are updating the uncollectible rate.  They are not, 
8 
however, proposing to change the current methodology of charging 
9 
uncollectible expense to firm sales customers or to ESCOs that participate 
10 
in the POR program. 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
How do KEDNY and KEDLI propose to calculate uncollectible 
13 
expenses for the Rate Year and Data Years?  
14 
A. 
The Companies propose to update their net write-off rates using a three-year 
15 
aggregated average uncollectible rate based on the Companies’ actual 
16 
experience during the three-year period CY 2017-2019, as set forth in 
17 
Exhibit ___ (CP-3), Schedule 1, and Exhibit ___ (CP-4), Schedule 1, for 
18 
KEDNY and KEDLI. 
19 
 
20 
Page 66 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 69 ---
Q. 
Why are calendar years 2017-2019 being used as the historic basis 
1 
period for determining the Companies’ uncollectible expense? 
2 
A. 
The prohibition of terminations of customers for non-payment during the 
3 
COVID-19 pandemic resulted in anomalous uncollectible rates during the 
4 
2020-2022 period.  As a result, the level of uncollectible expense over the 
5 
past three years does not present a fair or plausible estimate of the 
6 
Companies’ uncollectibles in the Rate Year and Data Years.  Using the 
7 
2017-2019 period to determine the uncollectible expense is more likely to 
8 
result in a level of expense that will be experienced in the Rate Year and 
9 
Data Years. 
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Why are KEDNY and KEDLI proposing an uncollectible rate based 
12 
on the three-year aggregated average?  
13 
A. 
The Companies propose to use a three-year aggregated average because a 
14 
multi-year average mitigates the impact of external factors that can occur 
15 
in any given year.  This includes the impacts of weather and the economy, 
16 
which can have an acute effect on consumption and supply cost.  Use of 
17 
a three-year period to calculate the net write-off rate is also consistent with 
18 
the methodology used in the 2019 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases and 
19 
the 2020 NMPC Rate Case. 
20 
 
21 
Page 67 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 70 ---
Q. 
What is the forecast uncollectible expense for KEDNY and KEDLI for 
1 
the Rate Year and Data Years? 
2 
A. 
Using the three-year aggregated average uncollectible rate of 0.8388 
3 
percent for KEDNY and 0.6076 percent for KEDLI, and multiplying that 
4 
rate by each company’s respective forecast retail revenues (including late 
5 
payment charge revenue and excluding power generation revenues), the 
6 
uncollectible expense for KEDNY and KEDLI for the Rate Year is $18.66 
7 
million and $9.25 million, respectively.  The uncollectible expense for 
8 
KEDNY and KEDLI for Data Years 1, 2 and 3 are shown in Exhibit__ 
9 
(RRP-3), Schedule 31.   
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Are the Companies proposing a reconciliation mechanism for 
12 
uncollectible expense?   
13 
A. 
Yes.  The Companies are proposing a two-way uncollectible expense 
14 
reconciliation mechanism that would reconcile the uncollectible expense 
15 
recovered in base rates against actual write-offs.  Under the mechanism, the 
16 
Companies will reconcile actual uncollectible expense (i.e., net write-offs) 
17 
to the amount reflected in base rates.  The reconciliation will include the 
18 
commodity portion of uncollectible expense that is recovered through the 
19 
MFC and the amounts recovered from ESCOs through the POR discount. 
20

Q. 
Please describe how KEDNY and KEDLI manage the collections 
1 
process to minimize uncollectible expense. 
2 
A. 
KEDNY and KEDLI use a full suite of collection activities and strategies 
3 
to manage the collection process and minimize uncollectible expenses. 
4 
These activities and strategies are employed throughout the Companies’ 
5 
delinquency management process, which consists of four key components: 
6 
Account Initiation, Account Management, Field Collections, and Final Bill 
7 
Management. 
8 
 
9 
Account Initiation is the point at which the Companies establish a 
10 
service contract with the customer applying for service.  Prior to 
11 
initiating service, the Companies confirm the identification of each 
12 
applicant. If an applicant has outstanding debt with one of the 
13 
Companies within the last six years, they are required to pay the balance 
14 
owed or establish a payment agreement prior to obtaining service.  The 
15 
past debt is then transferred to the new active account under the terms of 
16 
the new payment agreement. 
17 
 
18 
Account Management is the method the Companies use to actively 
19 
monitor customer payment history.  The Companies manage collection 
20 
accounts using a behavioral scoring mechanism referred to as a Portfolio 
21 
Page 71 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 74 ---
Management Package (“PMP”).  Each customer account in arrears is 
1 
evaluated and scored using PMP.  The output from the model, which is 
2 
reviewed and updated at regular intervals, assists the Companies in 
3 
determining the appropriate collections actions based on the customer’s 
4 
past payment behavior.  Customers are divided into five risk groups, and 
5 
each group is assigned the most cost-effective treatment path that is 
6 
likely to be successful.  In prioritizing the accounts in the portfolio, the 
7 
Companies seek to identify lower risk customers that will likely self-cure 
8 
and higher risk customers requiring more assertive collection measures.  
9 
This process attempts to tailor the response to the specific circumstances 
10 
of the individual customer and attempts to ensure that the most cost-
11 
effective steps are taken to mitigate the Companies’ uncollectible expense.  
12 
Additionally, the Companies have increased the use of targeted outbound 
13 
calling campaigns to improve effectiveness in encouraging customers 
14 
to act regarding their arrears. 
15 
 
16 
Field Collections begins after a final notice has been issued to the 
17 
customer.  Field Collections are the actions taken by the Companies to 
18 
collect payments in a manner that is mindful of customer rights, 
19 
protections, and safety.  As a last resort, service may be terminated because 
20 
of non-payment.  Final Bill Management is the process of collecting 
21 
Page 72 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 75 ---
final or written-off accounts with the assistance of outside collection 
1 
agencies.  The Companies utilize a tool that enables them to manage 
2 
this collections work stream and to track and execute strategies to 
3 
maximize resources and minimize costs. 
4 
 
5 
The Companies also conduct ongoing email campaigns for residential 
6 
customers with an arrears balance of at least $50 pending for 60 or more 
7 
days.  Reminder emails are sent on a weekly basis to customers that meet 
8 
the criteria. 
9 
 
10 
For KEDLI, the Company also executes a collection residential account 
11 
management strategy directed at high arrears residential accounts.  This 
12 
strategy focuses on reaching out to customers by phone to find ways to 
13 
reduce arrears and decrease terminations using alternative methods when 
14 
normal collection paths have been unsuccessful.  While uncollectible 
15 
expense and the Companies’ mitigation efforts are a significant focus, bad 
16 
debt expense in a given year is also driven by a number of factors outside 
17 
the Companies’ control - including weather, commodity cost, customer 
18 
protections, and economic health. 
19 
 
20 
Page 73 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel

Q. 
How were the incentive measures calculated in the 2016 KEDNY and 
4 
KEDLI Rate Cases? 
5 
A.

6

7

8

11 
percentages for KEDNY were applied to KEDLI’s data to determine the 
12 
incentive.

9 
VI. 
Residential Credit Card Fees 
10 
Q. 
Please describe the Companies’ current options regarding payments 
11 
made using credit and debit cards (collectively “CC/DC”)? 
12 
A. 
Currently, customers can pay their utility bills via CC/DC in several ways. 
13 
Customers can pay using a card on the Companies’ website or through the 
14 
Page 79 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 82 ---
Companies’ mobile app.  Customers enrolled in electronic billing receive 
1 
an email with a link that takes them to a site where they can pay via CC/DC.  
2 
Finally, customers can pay by phone using the automated telephone system 
3 
(i.e., IVR).  Residential customers who use one of these options to make a 
4 
CC/DC payment incur a transaction fee of $1.75 for payment amounts up 
5 
to $1,300. The customer can make up to five payments in a 30-day period. 
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Do the Companies propose to change how CC/DC payments are 
8 
handled? 
9 
A. 
Yes.  The Companies propose to include in base rates the estimated cost to 
10 
recover the fees incurred by KEDNY and KEDLI when residential 
11 
customers make CC/DC payments.  This is referred to as a “no-fee model.”  
12 
The no-fee model would eliminate the per-transaction cost to customers 
13 
paying by CC/DC by shifting the cost into base rates. 
14 
 
15 
Q. 
Why are the Companies making this proposal for a no-fee model? 
16 
A. 
According to Federal Reserve payment data collected in 2020, there was a 
17 
decline in card usage due to COVID.  Prior to the COVID-19 pandemic, the 
18 
Companies saw steady increases in card usage.  The Companies 
19 
experienced a decline during COVID, but volumes have begun to increase 
20 
back to the standard yearly increase experienced pre-COVID.  KEDNY saw 
21 
Page 80 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 83 ---
a 35 percent increase in CC/DC volumes from 2020 to 2021.  KEDLI 
1 
volumes increased at a lower rate but are now trending back to the typical 
2 
annual year over year increases. 
3 
 
4 
In response to surveys, customers have indicated a preference for CC/DC 
5 
payments with no fee.  Customers have become accustomed to paying 
6 
without convenience fees when shopping online or paying for services like 
7 
their mobile phones.  Moving to a no-fee model will help meet customer 
8 
expectations for improved payment options and enhance customer ease and 
9 
overall experience.  In addition, the Companies believe transitioning to a 
10 
no-fee model will benefit customers who receive public assistance benefits 
11 
via pre-paid debit cards.  Under the current model, these customers can pay 
12 
their utility bill with their pre-paid debit card but must use a portion of the 
13 
benefits to cover the vendor fee for CC/DC payments, resulting in an 
14 
economic disadvantage.  Adopting a no-fee model will eliminate the need 
15 
for a portion of public assistance benefits to be used to pay this 
16 
administrative fee.  A no-fee model would provide customers options to 
17 
enroll in recurring CC/DC payments.  Also, with a no-fee model the 
18 
Companies can apply to VISA for a utility rate that would potentially reduce 
19 
the fees even lower than current rates for all accepted credit cards.  
20 
Page 81 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 84 ---
Additional details concerning the Companies’ no-fee model proposal are set 
1 
forth in Exhibit __ (CP-5). 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Has the Commission approved utility proposals to shift to the no-fee 
4 
model? 
5 
A. 
Yes.  The Commission has approved similar proposals for New York State 
6 
Electric and Gas Corporation, Rochester Gas and Electric Corporation, 
7 
Central Hudson Gas and Electric Corporation, and Orange and Rockland 
8 
Utilities.  The Companies’ proposal is similar to other utilities’ practices. 
9 
 
10 
Q. 
What are the Companies’ estimated total annual costs resulting from a 
11 
transition to the no-fee model? 
12 
A. 
Using existing per transaction costs for CC/DC payments, the estimated 
13 
annual costs for KEDLI associated with the new program would be $1.255 
14 
million, $1.883 million, $2.825 million, and $4.237 million for the Rate 
15 
Year, Data Year 1, Data Year 2, and Data Year 3, respectively.  The 
16 
estimated annual costs for KEDNY would be $3.174 million, $4.761 
17 
million, $7.142 million, and $10.712 million for the Rate Year, Data Year 
18 
1, Data Year 2, and Data Year 3, respectively. Details on the calculation of 
19 
estimated costs are provided in Exhibit       (RRP-3, Schedule 40). 
20 
 
21 
Page 82 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 85 ---
Q. 
Do the Companies anticipate seeing an increase in payments made via 
1 
CC/DC under a no-fee model? 
2 
A. 
Yes.  Based on an analysis involving National Grid’s current third-party 
3 
payment processor and the Companies’ current volumes of CC/DC 
4 
payments an increase is anticipated in overall CC/DC usage.  The 
5 
Companies have seen an average of a 12 percent increase for KEDNY and 
6 
a 19 percent increase for KEDLI since 2017, not including the impact of 
7 
COVID.  The no-fee model would attract more customers; therefore, the 
8 
Companies anticipate an additional increase on top of this.  The Companies 
9 
also anticipate an increase in CC/DC payments that are re-occurring.  
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Do the Companies believe a reconciliation mechanism is appropriate 
12 
for implementation of the no-fee model? 
13 
A. 
Yes.  Considering the uncertainty associated with the transaction fees and 
14 
potential increase in customers making CC/DC payments in response to the 
15 
no-fee model, the Companies believe a two-way deferral mechanism should 
16 
be put in place for the no-fee  model.  If the costs to provide the no-fee 
17 
model exceed the rate allowance, the Companies would defer the shortfall 
18 
for future recovery from customers, or alternatively, if the costs are less than 
19 
the rate allowance, the Companies would defer the excess for the benefit of 
20 
customers. As discussed in the testimony of the Rate Design Panel, the 
21 
Page 83 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 86 ---
Companies are proposing to include the reconciliation of the no-fee model 
1 
in the Rate Adjustment Mechanism (“RAM”). 
2 
 
3 
VII. 
Collection Replevin Costs  
4 
Q. 
Please describe the collection process used by the Companies to 
5 
disconnect service for non-payment. 
6 
A. 
Field Collections begin after a final notice has been issued to the 
7 
customer.  Field Collections are the actions taken by the Companies to 
8 
collect payments in a manner that is mindful of customer rights, 
9 
protections, and safety.  As a last resort, service may be terminated 
10 
because of non-payment.  If, while attempting to terminate service, access to 
11 
the meter is not available during a visit, a second field visit is issued.  If access 
12 
is still not available, the account will be referred for either a curb valve cut or 
13 
collection replevin. 
14 
 
15 
Collection replevin is the legal process used to gain access to a meter for 
16 
termination including notifications to the customer, securing a warrant/writ 
17 
to obtain access to the meter, and ultimately a field visit to serve the 
18 
warrant/writ that includes a field collector, sheriff/law enforcement and a 
19 
locksmith.  This is used as a last resort to obtain access to the meter.  
20 
 
21 
Page 84 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 87 ---
Q.

1 
Historic Test Year compare to other years. 
2 
A. 
Collection replevin costs in the Historic Test Year are well below normal 
3 
years as residential field activity did not resume after being paused during 
4 
COVID at KEDNY until June 2022 and at KEDLI until July 2022.  Because 
5 
collection replevin occurs after a field visit to terminate service, collection 
6 
replevin activity was limited in the first half of the Historic Test Year.  
7 
 
8

9 
to the COVID-19 pandemic, 2017, 2018 and 2019 as well as the costs 
10 
incurred during the three COVID-19 pandemic years including the Historic 
11 
Test Year.  
12

Q.

VIII. CRIS to CSS Conversion 
13 
Q. 
Is National Grid currently engaged in consolidating the customer 
14 
system used by its utility operating companies into a single system? 
15 
A. 
Yes.  As described throughout this testimony, the transformation of 
16 
National Grid’s key customer processes, systems and capabilities is critical 
17 
to ensuring that customer expectations can be met and existing levels of 
18 
customer satisfaction can be maintained in a constantly evolving 
19 
environment.  As one of the initial steps in this process, National Grid is 
20 
consolidating the Customer Response Information System (“CRIS”) used 
21 
Page 86 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 89 ---
by KEDNY and National Grid’s Massachusetts gas operating companies 
1 
into the Customer Service System (“CSS”) used by National Grid’s other 
2 
utility operating companies, including KEDLI and Niagara Mohawk. 
3 
 
4 
The conversion to CSS will result in a single customer information system 
5 
for all National Grid operating companies focusing on single invoicing and 
6 
receivables management.  The consolidation of the Companies’ customer 
7 
information systems will enable National Grid to meet customers’ changing 
8 
expectations with greater efficiency, reliability, scalability, and extensibility 
9 
for future products and services.  The flexibility afforded by the CSS system 
10 
will allow the Companies to make incremental changes to the system to 
11 
address individual customer experience issues as they arise. 
12 
The primary benefits that the Companies will obtain over time from 
13 
operating a single customer information system are more consistent 
14 
customer service through a unified customer experience, the development 
15 
of consistent employee processes, and the ability to eliminate duplicative 
16 
operational and support teams. 
17 
 
18 
Page 87 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 90 ---
Q. 
What are the incremental costs to the Companies associated with the 
1 
CSS conversion? 
2 
A. 
The costs of the CSS conversion are discussed by the IT Panel and are set 
3 
forth in Exhibit __ (ITDP-4). 
4 
 
5

6

IX. 
Energy Efficiency 
9 
Q. 
Please provide an overview of the Companies’ energy efficiency 
10 
programs. 
11 
A. 
The Companies offer a variety of gas energy efficiency programs for their 
12 
customers, as described in the 2019 – 2025 System Energy Efficiency Plan 
13 
(“SEEP”) filed with the Commission on October 31, 2022, in Cases 18-M-
14 
0084 and 15-M-0252 (“2022 SEEP Filing”), and subsequently updated in 
15 
quarterly revisions, filed most recently on March 31, 2023.  The 
16 
Companies’ energy efficiency programs are designed to support the 
17 
progression of market-based solutions and the penetration of emerging and 
18 
transformative technologies in New York, consistent with the CLCPA, the 
19 
NE:NY proceeding, and overall gas system modernization efforts.  The 
20 
Companies continue to strive to meet or exceed the current energy savings 
21 
Page 88 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 91 ---
goals established in the NE:NY proceeding while finding new opportunities 
1 
to reduce implementation and administration costs of their energy 
2 
efficiency programs.  Expanded energy efficiency offerings will take a more 
3 
holistic approach to delivering customer solutions that are accessible to a 
4 
wider range of customers, including those in Disadvantaged Communities, 
5 
and achieve energy savings in the most cost-effective manner possible.  The 
6 
portfolio of programs includes non-residential, residential, and multi-family 
7 
sector-specific offerings designed to serve the diverse needs of all three 
8 
broad market sectors.   
9 
 
10 
During the past three years, the Companies’ overall gas energy efficiency 
11 
program processed more than one million applications and helped 
12 
customers save approximately 3.73 million dekatherms.  With the ramp-up 
13 
in dekatherm savings expected through 2025 in the NE:NY Order, it is clear 
14 
that new and innovative programs will be required to meet not only these 
15 
goals, but also those that are to be established in the NE:NY Interim Review 
16 
(described more fully below).  Continued growth and expansion of 
17 
midstream and upstream programs will increase the volume of participants.  
18 
Work with trade allies such as home improvement contractors, plumbers 
19 
and insulation installers will position more customers and contractors to 
20 
participate in our programs.  Innovative program structures will open more 
21 
Page 89 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 92 ---
potential financing programs.  In addition, a deeper, more holistic clean 
1 
energy transition process with larger customers could help to unlock 
2 
significant potential savings.  In the Companies’ Annual Demand-Side 
3 
Management filings, the Companies have demonstrated the impact that 
4 
building envelope programs can exhibit, as well as the need for additional 
5 
resources to ensure the programs’ success. 
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Have the energy efficiency programs helped the Companies achieve 
8 
their billed gas usage reduction goals? 
9 
A. 
Yes.  In the 2019 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, the Companies agreed 
10 
to operate their systems with the objective of achieving one half of one 
11 
percent reductions in billed gas usage as compared to the levels in the then-
12 
current forecast.  The energy efficiency programs are one of the primary 
13 
means by which the Companies can enable customers to reduce their annual 
14 
gas consumption.  In the Companies’ gas load forecast, a certain amount of 
15 
energy efficiency savings is assumed to be delivered by the Companies’ 
16 
energy efficiency programs; energy efficiency savings achieved over and 
17 
above that amount would contribute to the attainment of the billed gas usage 
18 
reduction goals for the lifetime of the energy efficiency measures.    
19 
 
20 
Page 90 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 93 ---
Q. 
Please describe the New Efficiency: New York Order. 
1 
A. 
NE:NY is a statewide, comprehensive energy efficiency strategy that was 
2 
initially outlined in a Whitepaper developed by DPS Staff and NYSERDA, 
3 
and adopted by the Commission in its “Accelerated Efficiency Order” 
4 
issued on December 13, 2018 in Case 18-M-0084.  The Accelerated 
5 
Efficiency Order established energy efficiency as the cornerstone of New 
6 
York’s national leadership on clean energy and climate change by 
7 
introducing a statewide goal of 185 trillion British Thermal units 
8 
(“TBtu”) of customer-level energy reduction by 2025.  The Order also 
9 
established an iterative approach with immediate accelerated utility targets 
10 
and budgets for 2019-2020, and was followed by the Commission’s “The 
11 
Energy Efficiency and Electrification Order” (the “2020 NE:NY Order”), 
12 
issued January 16, 2020 in Case 18-M-0084, which authorized the energy 
13 
efficiency budgets and targets through 2025 to support achievement of the 
14 
State’s goals.  For LMI customers, the 2020 NE:NY Order commits 20 
15 
percent of incremental energy efficiency funding to support LMI energy 
16 
efficiency programs.  Consistent with these orders, National Grid has been 
17 
fully engaged in the clean energy future by creating a suite of energy 
18 
efficiency programs under NE:NY. 
19 
 
20 
Page 91 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 94 ---
Q. 
Please describe the NE:NY Order Interim Review. 
1 
A. 
As required by the 2020 NE:NY Order, the utility energy efficiency 
2 
portfolios must undergo “interim reviews.”  These reviews are intended to 
3 
provide an opportunity to assess progress to date and consider modifications 
4 
that will improve the management of the portfolios, increase the 
5 
effectiveness of the programs, and ensure alignment with evolving state 
6 
policies.  The interim review process is currently underway, and it is 
7 
expected that the Commission will issue an order in mid-to-late 2023 with 
8 
updated energy efficiency program budgets and targets. 
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Describe the Companies’ energy efficiency efforts over the term of the 
11 
current rate plans. 
12 
A. 
At the beginning of the period, the Companies’ efforts focused on energy 
13 
efficient furnaces and heating and cooking equipment.  More recently, 
14 
because the Companies are not positioned to deliver electrification options 
15 
as gas-only utilities, the approach has shifted focusing on building envelope 
16 
enhancements (i.e., weatherization) and the associated long-lived and 
17 
durable usage and peak reductions that may be obtained.  This focus is 
18 
consistent with the Commission’s direction in the 2020 NE:NY Order and 
19 
the Climate Action Council’s (“CAC”) Final Scoping Plan.  The Companies 
20 
launched weatherization programs late in calendar year 2021.  In 2022, The 
21 
Page 92 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 95 ---
Residential Weatherization Program in KEDLI and KEDNY reacted to 
1 
market feedback resulting in improvements to the program and the customer 
2 
experience.  These changes increased customer and contractor participation, 
3 
resulting in serving over 2,000 customers from October 2022 to March, 
4 
2023 compared to 25 customers served from October 2021 to March 2022.  
5 
This success has continued in 2023 with high interest from customers and 
6 
contractors who want to become Approved Program Partners.   
7 
 
8 
The Companies anticipate serving around 3,000 customers this year, with 
9 
that number continuing to grow as more contractors submit project 
10 
applications.  To continue scaling these programs to deliver meaningful 
11 
reductions in customers’ energy usage, the Companies are using unspent 
12 
funds from prior years, and planning to pull forward funding that was 
13 
allocated for NE:NY 2024 and 2025 annual energy efficiency budgets.  
14 
With these efforts, however, the Companies are forecasting to spend the 
15 
entire budget sooner than 2025 to achieve accelerated benefits, which leaves 
16 
a potential funding gap.  As a result, the Companies are considering 
17 
opportunities (e.g., the NE:NY Interim Review) to propose additional 
18 
energy efficiency funding to continue delivering these meaningful programs 
19 
at scale in 2024 and 2025. 
20

5 
Q. 
Please describe the proposed Weatherization Health & Safety Program 
6 
and the associated costs.  
7 
A. 
The Weatherization Health & Safety Program will enable remediation of 
8 
health and safety barriers to weatherization, at no cost to the customer.  This 
9 
effort is needed to allow LMI customers and customers in Disadvantaged 
10 
Communities to access weatherization services more equitably, because 
11 
weatherization often involves significant home construction projects.  
12 
Barriers can include carbon monoxide hazards, mold, pests, insufficient 
13 
wiring or ventilation, plumbing problems, blocked access to spaces in the 
14 
home, unsafe appliances, and unsafe condition of the customer-owned gas 
15 
line.  Over the last fiscal year, the Companies began providing these 
16 
remediation and removal services as a pilot, using National Grid 
17 
shareholder funding.  During the pilot, remediation actions include 
18 
installation of ventilation fans, installation of carbon monoxide detectors, 
19 
tuning natural gas equipment, septic and plumbing troubleshooting, roof 
20 
Page 99 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 102 ---
replacement, removal of mold and asbestos, pest control, moisture control, 
1 
and correcting potential electrical hazards. 
2 
 
3 
KEDNY and KEDLI administered the pilot program in somewhat different 
4 
ways.  In KEDLI’s case, the contractor responsible for installing the energy 
5 
efficiency measures hires all the necessary installation or repair contractors 
6 
to remediate one or all issues at a customer’s premises and then invoices the 
7 
Company upon completion.  Initially, these activities are identified through 
8 
the energy audit, performed as part of the application for the KEDLI HEAT 
9 
program.  When health and safety barriers are identified during the audit, 
10 
then they are prioritized, and contractors are hired to perform remediation.  
11 
Upon completion, the actual weatherization will commence.  In KEDNY’s 
12 
case, it coordinates with and provides funding to NYSERDA for the 
13 
program, which in turn covers the necessary health and safety items as part 
14 
of its EmPower projects.  
15 
 
16 
Because the pilot program has proven beneficial for the uptake of 
17 
weatherization in Disadvantaged Communities served by the Companies, 
18 
the Companies propose to continue offering the program in the Rate Year 
19 
and beyond.  The program will operate as a companion to NYSERDA’s 
20 
EmPower Plus and the KEDLI HEAT programs and allow continued 
21 
Page 100 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 103 ---
progress in the Companies’ approach to addressing these types of barriers, 
1 
enabling better service for LMI customers and those living in 
2 
Disadvantaged Communities.  The Companies will track the health and 
3 
safety spending that allows weatherization participation.  The requested 
4 
budgets will be designated to serve LMI and Disadvantaged Community 
5 
customers (those being served by EmPower or KEDLI HEAT).  The 
6 
proposed costs to run the program are $0.750 million per year in KEDNY 
7 
and $1.75 million per year in KEDLI, as set forth on Exhibit ___ (CP-6), 
8 
Schedule 1.

11

17 
key barriers to engagement in energy efficiency programs and strategies to 
18

19

1
