3 
testing is not limited to serving Disadvantaged Communities and has a 
4 
broader focus around utility-customer relationships.  The Companies’ 
5 
proposed program is solely focused on engagement in Disadvantaged 
6 
Communities in KEDNY’s and KEDLI’s service territories.  Research 
7 
questions and strategies would align directly with enrolling customers in the 
8 
Companies’ programs during the rate plan.  A combination of in-house and 
9 
vendor resources will implement the pilot.  The outcomes will ensure future 
10 
outreach and engagement uses the most effective channels.  In addition, 
11 
information gained from the program will help the Companies’ sales and 
12 
marketing teams use communications that are most meaningful to and 
13 
aligned with customers residing in Disadvantaged Communities.   
14 
15 
The cost to deliver this program is $0.500 million (split between KEDNY 
16 
and KEDLI) in the Rate Year and shown in the direct testimony of the 
17 
Revenue Requirement Panels, Exhibit   (RRP-3), Schedule 40.  The 
18 
Companies are additionally proposing an enhanced marketing and outreach 
19 
program for LMI customers discussed previously that will be focused on 
20 
strategies to engage LMI customers to participate in bill assistance

programs specifically, but will coordinate efforts with the Disadvantaged

Q. 
Please describe the proposed Language Access Program and the 
7 
associated costs. 
8 
A. 
Experience indicates that language access is a significant barrier to 
9 
customer participation in energy efficiency programs for LMI customers 
10 
and customers in Disadvantaged Communities.  Currently, inquiries related 
11 
to energy efficiency programs are directed to external implementation 
12 
vendors, and not to the in-house call center.  Implementation vendors 
13 
interface directly with contractor networks and recruit and enroll customers 
14 
in the Companies’ energy efficiency programs.  In general, most vendors 
15 
do not have in-house capability to provide the necessary language services.   
16 
 
17 
A strategic approach is necessary to identify best practices and begin to 
18 
deploy communications specific to the Companies’ energy efficiency 
19 
programs in multiple languages.  Under the proposed pilot program, the 
20 
Companies’ program teams will work with interested stakeholders to 
21 
Page 103 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 106 ---
provide a mix of language accessibility services to apply to websites, flyers, 
1 
brochures, direct mailers, and advertisements, when appropriate.  The 
2 
Companies will utilize in-house marketing services and/or specialty 
3 
vendors.  Services may also include remote translation and translation of 
4 
written materials.  Because there are no direct savings associated with the 
5 
pilot program, the Companies propose to fund it outside of the NE:NY 
6 
program.  The purpose of evaluating new strategies using non-NE:NY funds 
7 
is to vet these techniques before looking at any embedded, full-scale 
8 
implementation using program administration budgets, ensuring efficient 
9 
program delivery and cost effectiveness.  This will ensure that the 
10 
Companies’ attempt to increase participation will not indirectly misalign the 
11 
run rates of individual programs.  A similar effort has been undertaken for 
12 
National Grid’s Massachusetts energy efficiency programs and has shown 
13 
a positive effect.  The cost to implement this program is a $0.250 million 
14 
one-time cost in the Rate Year, shared equally between KEDNY and 
15 
KEDLI, as shown in the direct testimony of the Revenue Requirements 
16 
Panels, Exhibit  (RRP-3), Schedule 40.  The pilot solution will be conducted 
17 
simultaneously with the implementation of any existing, more mature 
18 
solutions.  The results will inform the Companies understanding of the best 
19 
approach and ideal fit to incorporate into the respective programs.

Q. 
Please describe the proposed Digital Building Replica Pilot and the 
4 
associated costs. 
5 
A. 
The Companies propose to create a data model of a building that functions 
6 
identically to the physical buildings being modeled, which will enable the 
7 
Companies to help customers understand the energy impact of various 
8 
building decisions.  The replica will also provide customers with greater 
9 
ability to plan energy efficiency upgrades without (or in advance of) hiring 
10 
an external vendor.  The model will be created for three to four school 
11 
buildings within Disadvantaged Communities to predict energy savings 
12 
from participation in the Companies’ energy efficiency programs.  The pilot 
13 
aims to inform: (i) the types of measures that are most cost-effective and 
14 
yield the most savings for large buildings; (ii) whether a more universal 
15 
model could be created to serve a wider range of customers, allowing them 
16 
to plan and initiate efficiency upgrades they may have otherwise been 
17 
unaware of; (iii) whether, from a financial perspective, the development of 
18 
a building-specific model is cost effective to assist commercial and 
19 
industrial (“C&I”) customers in Disadvantaged Communities in planning 
20 
potential energy efficiency upgrades, and (iv) whether utility incentives 
21 
Page 105 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 108 ---
would support greater energy efficiency penetration.  The proposal is a 
1 
combination of an energy audit and building management system.  The 
2 
associated cost to run the pilot is a $0.5 million one-time cost in the Rate 
3 
Year, shared between KEDNY and KEDLI at a 40-60 percent split, 
4 
respectively.   
5 
 
6 
Additionally, the Companies will attempt to work with Con Edison and 
7 
PSE&G Long Island, in their respective territories, to incorporate an electric 
8 
component into the model, allowing customers to receive a holistic energy 
9 
assessment, rather than a gas-focused assessment.  Additional information 
10 
concerning this program is set forth in Exhibit ___ (CP-6), Schedule 4.  The 
11 
costs for this program are set forth in the direct testimony of the Revenue 
12 
Requirement Panels, Exhibit ___ (RRP-3), Schedule 40.

Q. 
Are there any proposed system upgrades that will benefit energy 
15 
efficiency?  
16 
A. 
Yes.  The following system upgrades are proposed to support the 
17 
Companies’ energy efficiency programs:   
18 
1. Clean Energy 2.0 – The Companies propose to replace InDemand (the 
19 
Companies’ demand-side management system) with a system known as 
20 
Clean Energy 2.0.  Currently, InDemand tracks all energy efficiency 
21 
Page 106 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 109 ---
program expenditures and savings in a detailed manner and the data is used 
1 
for both internal and external reporting.  InDemand was, however, 
2 
implemented more than 20 years ago and is composed of fragmented 
3 
architecture.  Maintenance is labor intensive, difficult to resource, and 
4 
costly.  The new system is key to allowing the Companies to keep up with 
5 
the increasing demand for program delivery.  It will greatly reduce cycle 
6 
time, make energy efficiency less dependent on program outsourcing, and 
7 
simplify system maintenance.  This Software as a Service platform is best 
8 
in class among utilities and comes preconfigured with the Companies’ most 
9 
essential requirements.  Additional information concerning the costs of this 
10 
system upgrade is provided in the IT&D Panel’s testimony.  In addition, as 
11 
discussed more fully below, the Companies are proposing to add one FTE 
12 
to assist with the operation of this system. 
13 
 
14 
2. Green Button Connect – The Companies are also proposing to implement 
15 
Green Button Connect.  With the Green Button Connect interface, 
16 
customers will be able to choose to share their energy usage and billing data 
17 
with authorized third parties.  Once a customer authorizes a third-party to 
18 
have access to their data, Green Button Connect facilitates computer-to-
19 
computer communication and provides a standardized protocol to provide 
20 
the third parties with access.  Customers can automate the process and 
21 
Page 107 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 110 ---
securely authorize the Company and designated third parties to send and 
1 
receive data on their behalf.  This approach is also consistent with the 
2 
Commission’s “Order Adopting Accelerated Energy Efficiency Targets” 
3 
issued on December 13, 2018 in Case 18-M-0084, which directed near-term 
4 
actions to enable access to energy-related data.  The procurement and initial 
5 
setup of the Green Button Connect interface for KEDNY and KEDLI will 
6 
be handled by the IT&D organization.  As set forth below, the Companies 
7 
propose one half of an FTE in the Customer Enablement organization to 
8 
support Green Button Connect implementation.  The Green Button Connect 
9 
upgrade costs are set forth in the IT&D Panel’s testimony.

Q. 
In addition to the proposed programs are the Companies requesting 
12 
funding for additional FTEs to enhance their energy efficiency efforts?  
13 
A. 
Yes.  Since the issuance of the 2020 NE:NY Order, which increased the 
14 
energy efficiency savings targets by 449 percent and 296 percent for KEDLI 
15 
and KEDNY, respectively, for 2021 through 2025, the focus of energy 
16 
efficiency programs has changed from programs that administer monetary 
17 
incentives to install more efficient gas furnaces and appliances to a far more 
18 
labor-intensive emphasis on enhancing building envelopes (i.e., 
19 
weatherization).  As the CAC Final Scoping Plan recognized, challenges 
20 
Page 108 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 111 ---
abound in convincing owners of homes and businesses to adopt 
1 
weatherization measures: 
2 
Financial incentive programs will need to scale up 
3 
dramatically to motivate millions of homeowners and 
4 
building owners to install high-efficiency electric heat 
5 
pumps and make energy efficiency improvements such as 
6 
sealing air leaks, adding insulation, and using building 
7 
controls. Building envelope sealing and weatherization 
8 
measures improve comfort (reducing drafts, cold surfaces, 
9 
and noise pollution) and reduce risk of pests and mold; 
10 
moreover, to promote healthy homes, weatherization needs 
11 
to include appropriate ventilation systems and must take 
12 
place after any pre-existing safety and health issues (such as 
13 
mold, mustiness, or high levels of formaldehyde and other 
14 
air pollutants) are addressed.…Although many energy 
15 
efficiency upgrades improve housing quality and are cost-
16 
effective, these projects can be disruptive for occupants or 
17 
simply not a priority for owners. For most existing homes 
18 
and buildings, moreover, the current upfront costs of 
19 
building electrification and related energy efficiency 
20 
upgrades such as air sealing, insulation, and building 
21 
controls can be significantly higher than costs for replacing 
22 
fossil fuel equipment. (p. 192). 
23 
 
24 
The Final Scoping Plan goes on to discuss the efforts that are required to 
25 
make such programs successful, especially for our LMI customers in 
26 
Disadvantaged Communities: 
27 
Dedicated financial support programs for LMI households, 
28 
affordable and public housing, and Disadvantaged 
29 
Communities are essential to enable these households to 
30 
make and benefit from energy upgrades that improve 
31 
housing quality and comfort, with careful attention to 
32 
impacts on housing and energy affordability. For these 
33 
households and buildings, grant funding will need to cover 
34 
most or all the near-term cost premium for building 
35 
Page 109 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 112 ---
electrification 
and 
efficiency 
upgrades, 
considering 
1 
economic realities and to remedy unjust patterns of redlining 
2 
and underinvestment in Disadvantaged Communities. A 
3 
major investment of public funding, at a scale and along a 
4 
timeframe that aligns with the adoption of zero-emission 
5 
codes and standards, will be needed to decarbonize and 
6 
improve the quality of housing for LMI households, 
7 
affordable and public housing, and in Disadvantaged 
8 
Communities.  (p. 194). 
9 
 
10 
Given the challenges involved in running, administering, and promoting 
11 
this new effort, the Companies are proposing 14 additional FTEs to help 
12 
ensure the delivery of targets set through 2025 and beyond.  The Companies 
13 
current energy efficiency teams are exceeding their work capacities, and 
14 
with the further scaling of these programs, the Companies anticipate such

resources will be further strained with the significant expansion of energy

To deliver on the aggressive targets that have been set by the CLCPA, as 
18 
well as the NE:NY initiatives to be addressed in the Interim Review, the

22

23 
benefits of these programs.  The additional FTEs will focus specifically on

the need for expansion of deeper savings initiatives such as weatherization, 
25 
especially in the LMI and Disadvantaged Community sectors.  Although the

Companies’ initial efforts in transitioning programs to the more labor-

15 
X. 
Demand Response 
16 
Q. 
 Please describe the Companies’ firm gas demand response (“DR”) 
17 
portfolio. 
18 
A. 
The Companies’ firm gas DR portfolio is composed of four programs that 
19 
enable the Companies to help preserve system reliability and reduce 
20 
infrastructure costs by temporarily reducing end-use consumption of natural 
21 
Page 121 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 124 ---
gas during periods of peak-day and peak-hour demand on the gas 
1 
distribution system.  The DR portfolio achieves this by one of two primary 
2 
means: by encouraging customers to either temporarily cease their 
3 
consumption of natural gas in whole or in part (i.e., by shedding load) or to 
4 
shift some of their consumption of natural gas to non-peak hours (i.e., by 
5 
shifting load).   
6 
 
7 
The Companies’ Load Shedding program, in which C&I, institutional, and 
8 
multifamily customers either switch to a backup fuel source or curtail gas 
9 
demand entirely, falls into the former category.  The latter category is 
10 
composed of three programs: (i) Load Shifting, in which C&I, institutional, 
11 
and multifamily customers generally shift their usage to non-peak hours by 
12 
reducing their heating setpoints; (ii) the Bring Your Own Thermostat 
13 
(“BYOT”) program, in which residential customers enable a vendor to 
14 
temporarily reduce their heating setpoints via an opt-out mechanism; and 
15 
(iii) the Behavioral DR program, in which the Companies send out alerts 
16 
that ask customers to voluntarily reduce their gas usage.  Customers who 
17 
participate in the Load Shedding, Load Shifting, and BYOT programs can 
18 
earn incentives for enrollment and performance and help ensure the 
19 
reliability of the gas system. 
20 
 
21 
Page 122 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 125 ---
These programs were first launched as pilots in 2017 and have grown into 
1 
mature programs since that time.  They have proven effective at engaging a 
2 
wide variety of customers and at producing meaningful reductions in peak-
3 
hour and peak-day demand.   
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Are the Companies proposing any changes to the gas DR oversight and 
6 
reporting process? 
7 
A. 
Yes.  Under the current framework, the Companies make DR filings on June 
8 
15 of each year in Cases 20-G-0086 and 20-G-0087 and provide additional 
9 
information in an annual Demand-Side Management filing that is due on 
10 
July 1 of each year.  This dual reporting can: (i) lead to stakeholder 
11 
confusion, and (ii) create unnecessary reporting and oversight burdens.  As 
12 
such, the Companies propose that all gas DR-related reporting requirements 
13 
should be limited to what is required in Cases 20-G-0086 and 20-G-0087.  
14 
The Companies believe this will simplify the process for all stakeholders. 
15 
 
16 
Q. 
Are the Companies proposing to modify their existing firm gas DR 
17 
programs or add new DR programs? 
18 
A. 
No.  However, if the Companies do propose any such modifications or 
19 
additions, they will do so in their June 15th filings each year in Cases 20-G-
20 
0086 and 20-G-0087. 
21 
Page 123 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 126 ---
Q. 
How are the Companies currently recovering the non-labor operating 
1 
expense costs of their firm gas DR programs? 
2 
A. 
The Companies currently recover non-labor O&M program costs, which 
3 
consist primarily of capacity and performance incentive payments made to 
4 
customers and a very small amount of marketing and administrative costs 
5 
for the Load Shedding and BYOT program through the Demand Capacity 
6 
Surcharge Mechanism (“DCSM”).  The Companies recover 50 percent of 
7 
estimated costs for those programs through the DCSM beginning in the July 
8 
prior to each winter season, and then recover the remainder via a true-up in 
9 
a surcharge that begins the following July, thereby providing the ability to 
10 
recover DR costs that vary due to changes in enrollment levels, the number 
11 
of occasions that the Companies call for DR customers to modify their 
12 
usage, and customer performance.  Non-labor O&M costs for the Load 
13 
Shifting program are recovered through the Demand Response O&M 
14 
(“DROM”) component of the Delivery Rate Adjustment (“DRA”), after the 
15 
close of each winter season. 
16 
 
17 
Q. 
How do the Companies propose to recover non-labor operating expense 
18 
costs in future? 
19 
A. 
The Companies believe that recovering non-labor DR program costs 
20 
through a surcharge remains appropriate.  Such recovery is consistent with 
21 
Page 124 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 127 ---
the method approved in the 2020 NMPC Rate Case, and consistent with 
1 
how costs are recovered for electric DR programs throughout New York 
2 
State. 
3 
 
4 
Because the Companies are proposing to eliminate the DCSM after June 30, 
5 
2024, as discussed more fully below, the Companies propose to recover DR 
6 
non-labor O&M program costs through the DROM as of July 1, 2024.  
7 
Under the Companies’ proposal, the unrecovered DR costs incurred during 
8 
the winter of 2023-2024 would be recovered through the DROM surcharge 
9 
beginning July 1, 2024.  In addition, 100 percent of DR costs in each 
10 
subsequent winter season beginning with winter 2024-2025 would be 
11 
surcharged through the DROM following the conclusion of each winter 
12 
season, with the revised DROM surcharge taking effect on July 1.  The 
13 
Companies believe that this surcharge proposal will reduce administrative 
14 
complexity by eliminating the recovery of a portion of DR costs prior to the 
15 
winter period.

19

Q. 
Why are the Companies requesting recovery of capital program costs 
1 
for the firm gas DR programs? 
2 
A. 
The Companies install non-standard metering equipment at the sites of 
3 
customers who participate in the Load Shedding and Load Shifting 
4 
programs to obtain hourly usage data that cannot be obtained via the 
5 
Companies’ standard metering and is needed to evaluate customer 
6 
participation in the programs.  The majority of customers who have enrolled 
7 
in the program to date have such metering equipment installed, so the costs 
8 
being requested herein are for replacements of failed equipment and for new 
9 
equipment installed at the premises of customers who enroll in one of the 
10 
programs for the first time.  Without the installation and timely replacement 
11 
of such equipment, the Companies would not be able to measure demand 
12 
reductions during DR events or accurately calculate customer performance 
13 
payments.

17

18

20

21

program, which is the primary source of demand reductions provided by the 
1 
firm gas DR portfolio.  However, because many of the larger customers who 
2

3

4

5

6

7

8).

Q. 
What do the Companies estimate the GHG emission impacts of their 
13 
firm gas DR portfolio to be? 
14 
A. 
Because gas DR events are called at most a few times a year and for a few 
15 
hours at a time, their impact on overall GHG emissions is minimal, and 
16 
accordingly is not projected to have any appreciable effect on GHG 
17 
emissions. 
18 
 
19 
Page 128 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 131 ---
Q. 
Do the Companies have any other gas DR initiatives? 
1 
A. 
Yes, the Companies have offered non-firm (i.e., interruptible) rates, under 
2 
tariffs approved by the Commission, for many years.  These customers were 
3 
previously referred to as “interruptible” or “temperature-controlled,” but are 
4 
now referred to as “non-firm,” and the service class as a whole is sometimes 
5 
referred to as “non-firm demand response” because customers on the non-
6 
firm rate classes respond to signals from the Companies to reduce demand 
7 
during peak times.  Although both the firm DR programs and the non-firm 
8 
DR rate provide peak demand reductions by incentivizing customers to 
9 
reduce their usage of gas at specific times, they do so in very different ways 
10 
and should not be considered interchangeable. 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
What benefit does the non-firm DR rate class provide to the Companies 
13 
and their customers? 
14 
A. 
Non-firm DR customers provide sizeable and highly reliable peak-day and 
15 
peak-hour reductions, and as such are an essential component of providing 
16 
safe and reliable gas distribution service.  Approximately 145,000 
17 
dekatherms of design day customer volume is on non-firm DR rates. 
18 
 
19 
Customers on non-firm DR rates have the ability to switch to firm rates, 
20 
and, as a result of customers doing so, there has been significant reduction 
21 
Page 129 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 132 ---
in the number of customers on non-firm DR rates over the past decade.  In 
1 
part to assist in stemming this migration, several changes to the non-firm 
2 
DR tariffs were made in the 2019 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, 
3 
including providing non-firm DR customers eligibility for energy efficiency 
4 
incentives and deeper discounts to tail block delivery rates.  The Companies 
5 
have had significant success in implementing energy efficiency with non-
6 
firm DR customers since that change.  Nevertheless, levels of migration 
7 
continue to be a matter of concern – in 2022 alone, an estimated 7,400 
8 
dekatherms of design day volume migrated to firm rates.  The Companies 
9 
believe that it is essential to work with non-firm DR customers to ensure 
10 
that customers remain on non-firm DR rates to ensure system reliability and 
11 
reduce the need to build new gas infrastructure. 
12 
 
13 
Q. 
What efforts are the Companies making to address the migration of 
14 
non-firm DR customers to firm rates? 
15 
A. 
The Companies have made, and will continue to make, several efforts to 
16 
mitigate the migration of non-firm DR customers to firm rates.  Commercial 
17 
Sales Representatives and Account Managers conduct direct outreach to 
18 
each customer that requests to migrate, and marketing materials were 
19 
recently sent to all non-firm DR customers to make them better aware of 
20 
the features and advantages of the rate.  Additionally, to better understand 
21 
Page 130 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 133 ---
customers’ awareness of the features of the rate class and the relative weight 
1 
of the factors that might influence them to migrate, the Companies recently 
2 
conducted a survey, the results of which should enable the Companies to 
3 
better target their efforts. 
4

Yes.  The Companies propose a modest increase to the discounted 
7

8 
percent for Tier 1 and 60 percent for Tier 2 of the otherwise applicable firm 
9

10

11

12 
and System Performance Adjustment (“SPA”) reconciliation, which is 
13 
explained in the testimony of the Rate Design Panels.

5 
XI. 
Demand Capacity Surcharge Mechanism / Capacity Demand Metrics 
6 
Q. 
You earlier referred to the DCSM.  What is the derivation of that 
7 
surcharge? 
8 
A. 
The DCSM is a cost recovery mechanism established in the 2019 KEDNY 
9 
and KEDLI Rate Cases that effectuates the recovery of: (i) the revenue 
10 
requirements associated with the Long-Term Capital Capacity Projects that 
11 
are subject to the Capacity Demand Metrics, (ii) non-labor O&M costs of 
12 
the gas DR portfolio; and (iii) any energy efficiency funding incremental to 
13 
NE:NY-approved funds that the Companies request via the Annual 
14 
Demand-Side Management Filings.  Under the terms of the 2019 Joint 
15 
Proposal, the DCSM was reorganized as a “unique cost recovery 
16 
mechanism that is being established for the purpose of these rate 
17 
proceedings only” and the 2019 Joint Proposal further provided that there 
18 
was to be no “presumption that the DCSM will continue in subsequent rate 
19 
proceedings.” 
20 
 
21 
Page 133 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 136 ---
Q. 
What are the Capacity Demand Metrics? 
1 
A. 
The Capacity Demand Metrics are a set of five metrics related to reducing 
2 
peak gas demand through demand-side management that were established 
3 
in the 2019 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases (Joint Proposal, Section 5.3.2): 
4 
(i) energy efficiency, (ii) demand response, (iii) non-pipes/third-party 
5 
solutions, (iv) electrification and (v) leak-prone pipe, non-pipes alternatives 
6 
(“NPAs”).  Under the terms of the Joint Proposal approved in the 2019 
7 
KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, failure to meet the metric targets results 
8 
in the Companies being unable to fully recover certain costs of the Long-
9 
Term Capital Projects during the term of the current rate plans. 
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Is the Company proposing to eliminate the DCSM? 
12 
A. 
Yes.  The Companies are proposing to eliminate the DCSM effective July 
13 
1, 2024.   
14 
 
15 
With respect to the continuing recovery of energy efficiency and DR costs, 
16 
the Companies are proposing alternative means of recovery in lieu of the 
17 
DCSM.  Regarding incremental energy efficiency funding, the Companies 
18 
anticipate that the Interim Review currently under way as part of the NE:NY 
19 
proceeding will authorize funding levels necessary to achieve current and 
20 
future state-mandated energy efficiency goals, while continuing to allow 
21 
Page 134 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 137 ---
utilities the flexibility to shift funding across calendar years.  The 
1 
Companies believe such costs are best recovered in base rates rather than 
2 
being recovered in part through a surcharge.  However, to the extent that 
3 
base rates are established in this proceeding before any incremental costs 
4 
are authorized in the Interim Review, the Companies propose that a 
5 
surcharge be established (such as the proposed Rate Adjustment 
6 
Mechanism (“RAM”) discussed in the testimony of the Rate Design Panels) 
7 
to permit recovery of incremental energy efficiency costs.  Regarding 
8 
funding for non-labor operating expense for gas DR, as explained above, 
9 
the Companies propose using an existing surcharge mechanism (e.g., the 
10 
DROM) where the timing of recovery is closer to the actual expenditures. 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Do the Companies propose to continue the Capacity Demand Metrics? 
13 
A. 
No.  The Companies’ Capacity Demand Metrics were established solely for 
14 
the purpose of determining whether the Companies would be permitted to 
15 
recover the Long-Term Capital Capacity Projects in the 2019 KEDNY and 
16 
KEDLI Rate Cases.  The Companies’ peer gas and electric utilities are not 
17 
subject to such demand-side metrics, much less any that are tied to potential 
18 
penalties related to the recovery of prudently incurred capital expenditures. 
19 
 
20 
Page 135 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 138 ---
Q. 
Do the Companies nevertheless endeavor to achieve the goals of the 
1 
Capacity Demand Metrics if the metrics are eliminated? 
2 
A. 
Yes.  The Companies fully intend to continue to ensure that they are taking 
3 
aggressive actions to promote energy efficiency, demand response, non-
4 
pipes/third-party solutions, electrification and NPAs by continuing all 
5 
existing programs to provide beneficial outcomes.  The Companies have 
6 
demonstrated their commitment to those efforts throughout the course of 
7 
their current rate plans, and in their proposals to continue those efforts as 
8 
part of these rate filings, as well as in other proceedings such as the Gas 
9 
Planning Proceeding.  There is no need for the Commission to continue 
10 
Capacity Demand Metrics to ensure the Companies’ continued commitment 
11 
to these activities. 
12 
 
13 
Q. 
Please explain how the Companies have been referring potential 
14 
customers to electric utilities. 
15 
A. 
The Companies have focused their efforts at promoting electrification 
16 
referrals on customers who contact the Companies’ call centers seeking to 
17 
install new or upgraded gas connections.  The Companies have focused their 
18 
electrification efforts on these customers because when they contact the 
19 
Companies directly the customers are generally early in their decision 
20 
process and the Companies can engage directly with the customers. 
21 
Page 136 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 139 ---
At the same time, there are other pathways through which the Companies 
1 
receive requests for new or upgraded gas connections, such as directly from 
2 
contractors and plumbers on the customers’ behalf, and calls to the 
3 
Companies’ commercial account managers for entities involved in the 
4 
development of multifamily or C&I buildings. 
5 
 
6 
Q. 
Are the Companies investigating any enhancements to their current 
7 
referral efforts? 
8 
A. 
Yes.  First, the Companies continue to review the lead intake referral 
9 
process to determine whether improvements can be made.  Second, the 
10 
Companies are working to coordinate with the electric utilities to determine 
11 
the efficacy of the referrals (i.e., how many customers install a heat pump).  
12 
Third, the Companies are exploring whether there is a means by which 
13 
referrals made through other pathways can be done in such a way as to be 
14 
persuasive and effective.  Finally, the Companies will continue to work with 
15 
stakeholders to determine ways in which they can encourage more 
16 
customers to pursue electrification options.

XII.
Economic Development Programs 
1 
Q.
Please describe the current economic development grant programs 
2 
offered by the Companies in the KEDNY and KEDLI territories. 
3 
A.
KEDNY and KEDLI currently offer a portfolio of nine economic 
4 
development grant programs in their respective service territories.  
5 
Implemented in the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, the grants 
6 
provided through these programs support regional economic growth and 
7 
community development in the Companies’ service territories by: 
8 
1. Promoting sustainable growth and the revitalization of distressed
9 
communities by redeveloping vacant and underutilized buildings,
10 
commercial corridors and brownfield sites.  These programs include
11 
the Cinderella Program, the Industrial Building Redevelopment
12 
Program, and the Brownfield Redevelopment Assistance Program.
13 
2. Helping C&I customers invest in productivity improvements and
14 
resolve infrastructure issues that present barriers to their ability to
15 
remain and grow in the Companies’ service territories.  These
16 
programs include the Manufacturing Productivity Program and
17 
Capital Investment Incentive.
18 
3. Enabling the development and deployment of new technologies as
19 
alternatives to traditional natural gas infrastructure upgrades and
20 
supporting the entrepreneurial “ecosystem” for clean energy and
21 
Page 138 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 141 ---
other advanced technologies.  These programs include the 
1 
Economic Development and the Future of Heat, Sustainable Gas and 
2 
Economic Development, and Cleantech Incubation programs.   
3 
4. Providing matching grants to regional and local economic
4 
development organizations to encourage new investment and job
5 
creation by growing companies in the Companies’ service
6 
territories.  This program is the Cooperative Business Recruitment
7 
program.
8 
9 
Q.
What is the current level of funding under the Companies’ current rate 
10 
plans for these programs?   
11 
A.
The authorized funding levels are $2.25 million per year and $2.75 million 
12 
per year for KEDNY and KEDLI, respectively. 
13 
14 
Q.
Please describe the current treatment of economic development grant 
15 
expenditures compared to the amount of these expenditures reflected 
16 
in base rates. 
17 
A.
For both KEDNY and KEDLI, actual grant expenditures are reconciled 
18 
against the amounts included in rates, subject to a downward-only 
19 
reconciliation.  Any under-expenditures in a given year are carried forward 
20 
and reconciled at the end of KEDNY and KEDLI’s current rate plan.  In the 
21 
Page 139 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 142 ---
event of anticipated over expenditures, the Companies may petition the 
1 
Commission for deferral treatment of any amount in excess of the rate 
2 
allowances.  Each rate year of the current rate plan, prior Economic 
3 
Development deferral credits are amortized such that the net revenue 
4 
requirement reflected in rates for each Company is $500,000 per year.    
5 
6 
Q.
How much funding has been disbursed to KEDNY and KEDLI 
7

10 
disbursed to customers, $1.06 million for KEDNY and $0.77 million for 
11 
KEDLI.  An additional $2.70 million in funding is committed through 
12 
approved applications; $1.51 million for KEDNY and $1.19 million for 
13 
KEDLI.  These funds will be provided to customers when they complete 
14 
their approved economic development projects.

14 
15 
Q.
Please describe the programs the Companies are proposing to 
16 
discontinue.   
17 
A.
The Capital Investment Incentive Program provides grants up to $500,000 
18 
to help offset customer costs associated with upgrading natural gas 
19 
infrastructure to accommodate a business expansion or new construction 
20 
project.  The Industrial Building Redevelopment Program provides 
21 
Page 141 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 144 ---
matching grants up to $250,000 to developers of large, vacant or 
1 
underutilized buildings who need assistance in upgrading, segregating, or 
2 
otherwise making the property’s gas infrastructure suitable for occupancy 
3 
by multiple tenants.  
4 
5 
The Companies propose to stop accepting new applications for these 
6 
programs beginning on the effective date of the new rate plan.  For 
7 
applications already approved as of the effective date, the Companies 
8 
propose to provide the approved grant funding if and when the project is 
9 
completed as per the approved application.   
10 
11 
Q.
Please describe the other program modifications proposed by
12 
the Companies.   
13 
A.
The Companies are proposing the following program modifications for both 
14 
KEDNY and KEDLI: 
15 
1. For the Brownfield Redevelopment Assistance and Cinderella
16 
programs, the Companies propose to prioritize and provide
17 
additional funding for eligible projects located in Disadvantaged
18 
Communities.  Eligibility for priority consideration and additional
19 
funding will be based on the criteria and listing of Disadvantaged
20 
Communities finalized by the Climate Justice Working Group on
21 
Page 142 of 148
Testimony of The Customer Panel


--- Page 145 ---
March 27, 2023, consistent with the CAC Final Scoping Plan.  In 
1 
the event the disadvantaged community criteria and listing are 
2 
further modified, the program criteria will be revised to reflect the 
3 
most updated definition and listing of Disadvantaged Communities 
4 
issued by the Climate Justice Working Group at that time. 
5 
2. For the Cinderella program, the Companies propose to provide
6 
matching grants for commercial corridor improvements in
7 
Disadvantaged Communities, and to support the provision of
8 
economic opportunities to business districts within those areas.
9 
3. For the Manufacturing Productivity Program and the Cooperative
10 
Business Recruitment Program, the Companies propose to prioritize
11 
and provide additional funding for projects that involve the
12 
expansion, retention and attraction of customers in clean energy
13 
industry sectors and their associated supply chains, including green
14 
hydrogen, RNG, bioprocessing, geothermal, energy storage and
15 
offshore wind.
16 
4. For the Economic Development and the Future of Heat program, the
17 
Companies propose to include feasibility analyses as an eligible
18 
project type in the program’s “customer track.”  Currently, funding
19 
for feasibility analyses is restricted to the “community track” of this
20 
program.
21

Testimony of The Customer Panel

The Companies also propose several minor, clarifying wording changes to 
1 
the existing KEDNY and KEDLI programs.  A detailed description of each 
2

3 
eligibility requirements and all proposed modifications described above, is 
4

5 
6 
Q.
What is the Companies’ rationale for proposing these changes?   
7 
A.
A fundamental objective of the CAC’s Final Scoping Plan is to support 
8 
Disadvantaged Communities in the energy transition and ensure that they 
9 
are a focus in terms of targeting CLCPA-related funding and benefits, 
10 
including investments in economic development.  The Final Scoping Plan 
11 
also includes a “clean-tech-focused economic development plan” that 
12 
encourages private sector investment and the attraction of clean energy 
13 
related businesses to New York (pp. 427-428).  In addition, the Final 
14 
Scoping Plan highlights the correlation between the speed of end-use 
15 
customer adoption of non-fossil gas technologies and the speed with which 
16 
the gas system can be transitioned (p. 353).  Thus, the Companies’ proposed 
17 
program modifications align with the coordinated approach to prioritizing 
18 
Disadvantaged Communities and stimulating growth in the clean energy 
19 
economy that is required by the CLCPA. 
20 
21

Testimony of The Customer Panel

the amounts more than the aggregate four-year rate allowance.

Additionally, to reduce customer bill impacts, the Companies propose that, 
18

Justification for Residential Segment Strategy - Energy Equity Analyst 
1 FTE for KEDNY and 1 FTE for KEDLI  
Description: 
Increasing participation in programs from low to moderate income (LMI) customers and 
disadvantaged communities requires addressing accessibility constraints arising from a lack of 
economic and educational access.  
Proposal to add 2 FTEs to be Energy Equity Segment Analysts who will be subject matter experts 
on the provisions of service to LMI customers and Disadvantaged Communities.  The Analysts 
will develop strategy for the residential market by looking at it through the broad equity lens: 
Disadvantaged Communities, and LMI. These analysts will find the gaps in the offerings the 
Companies have now, design strategy tailored to close the gaps, and grow segment specific 
programs.  Additionally, they will create awareness for these programs while expanding the 
number of LMI customers in and Disadvantaged Communities who are aware of, and willing to 
participate in the Companies’ energy efficiency programs.  Their work will be critical to ensuring 
that the Companies have a plan to reduce energy burden and meet the CLCPA goal of 35-40% of 
the climate action benefits going to Disadvantaged Communities and other vulnerable customers.  
Without a focus and more support for LMI customers and Disadvantaged Communities it will be 
increasingly challenging (or potentially impossible) to achieve the Companies’ overall energy 
efficiency savings goals.  This commitment of FTE resources is consistent with National Grid’s 
and New York State’s vision of an equitable energy future.  By having Analysts that understand 
why these customers have not been participating in the programs, their challenges, and concerns, 


--- Page 155 ---
Exhibit ___ (CP-1) 
Schedule 1 
Page 2 of 4 
the Companies can develop strategy, products, and programs to reduce this burden and increase 
participation. 
Stats: 
Currently the Companies have 30 FTEs supporting ~155,038 LMI Customers in KEDNY/ 
KEDLI, which is a ratio of 1 FTE for every ~5,167 LMI Customers.  Currently the Companies 
have several employees wearing multiple hats and attending events in the community to serve our 
LMI customers – see chart below. 
LMI Program Participation: 
•
10% of the residential population is currently enrolled in LMI programs but there is
potential for 19% of the population to be enrolled in LMI programs
•
Throughout NY, approximately 20% of the total state population is eligible to participate
in National Grid’s LMI program.
Having resources that can focus on the strategic and administrative work, while others can 
focus on engaging with customers most in need, will help accelerate progress. 
LMI Customers by region and number of supporting FTEs:

Program Title: Energy Affordability Program FTEs 
Brief Description: 
The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid NY (“KEDNY”) and KeySpan Gas East 
Corporation d/b/a National Grid (“KEDLI”) (collectively, the “Companies”) are requesting to 
include 3 full-time equivalents (“FTEs”) to oversee the strategy, implementation and 
administration of the Energy Affordability Program (“EAP”).  
Program Justification: 
The responsibilities required to maintain the EAP’s current function and continue to strategize on 
program improvements and ways to enroll more customers exceed the available resources. With 
the new requirements from the Energy Affordability Proceeding and Working Group and the New 
York Governor’s State of the State, the focus on low-to-moderate income customers is increasing 
and more resources are necessary to not only perform the day-to-day tasks, but to continue to 
innovate and support the upcoming policy objectives being established.  
Program Costs 
National Grid is requesting recovery to hiring 3 new FTEs to support the EAP. 
Increase in FTEs: The responsibilities of the roles would include, but not be limited to: strategy 
and program design; administering file matches and manual enrollments; performing calculation 
of the discounts; ensuring documented procedures are kept current and changes communicated; 
supporting internal and external reporting; preparing internal and external audit requests; ensuring 
KEDLI HEAT referrals are made and data maintained for reporting; and ensuring that the strategy 
and implementation plans are made and executed for new energy affordability policy objectives.

Program Title: Consumer Advocacy 
Brief Description: 
The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid NY (“KEDNY”) and KeySpan Gas East 
Corporation d/b/a National Grid (“KEDLI”) (collectively, the “Companies”) are proposing to add 
five Consumer Advocates.  
The program costs include 5 full-time Consumer Advocates (2 KEDLI, 3 KEDNY) to assist LMI 
customers in obtaining more affordable energy by promoting programs and services to customers 
and agencies through various channels such as inbound referrals, outbound calls, on-site at 
agencies, using targeted outreach   to reach customers in need, and increasing engagement in 
communities by holding webinars, presentations, and Expos.  
Program Justification: 
Consumer Advocates assist LMI households in making their energy more affordable, by: 
•
Educating customers
•
Identifying unidentified low-income customers to increase EAP participation
•
Promote programs and services such as HEAP, EAP, Empower NY, personalized payment
plans, budget billing, etc.
•
Hold expos, presentations, and webinars
•
Using purchased income data to find areas of disadvantaged communities to serve, to make
outbound calls to customers based on specific criteria as a way to maximize efficiency
The Companies believe the number of customers potentially qualifying for LMI programs is in the 
range of approximately 690,000 across KEDNY and KEDLI service territory.  The additional 
Consumer Advocates are needed to interface with this large population of customers,  reaching a 
significantly greater proportion of eligible customers than existing resources allow, providing 1:1 
assistance for a greater number of customers, and enrolling a larger proportion in our programs.  
The addition of five full-time Consumer Advocates by KEDLI would enable outreach and 
assistance to an additional 4,800 to 6,000 households and enable KEDLI to provide approximately 
72 community outreach events annually in KEDLI’s service territory.    
Program Costs 
National Grid is requesting recovery for Consumer Advocacy to hire 5 new FTEs to support the 
program.  
Program Budget: The proposed budget will cover FTEs. 
Increase in FTEs: The additional FTEs will perform the duties of a Consumer Advocate; the 
increased number will allow the Companies to do more for customers and communities – 
expanding the Companies' reach.  As stated above the Advocates will be: 
•
Educating customers

Program Title: Clean Energy Advisors 
Brief Description: 
The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid NY (“KEDNY”) and KeySpan Gas East 
Corporation d/b/a National Grid (“KEDLI”) (collectively, the “Companies”) are proposing a Clean 
Energy Advisor program to assist low-to-moderate income (“LMI”) customers in accessing clean 
energy programs by informing them of the available opportunities and helping them navigate 
through the program processes such as applications and scheduling appointments. The Clean 
Energy Advisors would also be liaisons to the New York State Energy and Research Development 
Authority (“NYSERDA”) Regional Clean Energy Hubs.  
The program costs include 3 full-time equivalents (“FTEs”) to provide individualized assistance 
to LMI customers as they engage in clean energy programs and interface with NYSERDA and 
their Regional Clean Energy Hubs and communities. 
Program Justification: 
There are many barriers for LMI customers to participating in clean energy programs such as lack 
of awareness and information surround clean energy programs, technologies and benefits, 
confusion and time constraints surrounding the process for participating in clean energy programs 
such as filling out applications, income qualifications, scheduling services, understanding project 
scopes and needing to access multiple programs or funding sources to complete the full project. 
The goal of this program is to provide guidance to customers to help them overcome those barriers 
and encourage more LMI customers to participate in clean energy programs. Additionally, with 
the roll-out of NYSERDA’s Regional Clean Energy Hubs it is important for the Companies to stay 
closely connected to and support NYSERDA, the Regional Clean Energy Hubs and the 
communities they serve and ensure coordination and continuity amongst the organizations.  
The Clean Energy Advisors are an important step to providing the type of support LMI customers 
may need to access clean energy programs and aligns with New York State’s goals set forth in the 
New Efficiency: New York Proceeding that emphasizes the importance to LMI energy efficiency 
programs and dedicates 20% of incremental funding to LMI customers. Their activities will also 
help further investments in Disadvantaged Communities which aligns with the Climate Leadership 
and Community Protection Act (“CLCPA”) requirement of at least 35% of the benefits from 
investments  
The NENY Order emphasizes the importance of LMI energy efficiency programs and dedicates 
20% of all incremental funding to LMI customers. The CLCPA requires at least 35% of benefits 
of clean energy be directed to underserved communities. The Energy Affordability Proceeding 
additionally aims to keep household energy burden at or below 6% and weatherization and energy 
efficiency is one of the most effective ways to permanently lower customer energy bills. These 
resources will also help to support the achievement of the LMI energy efficiency targets and 
proposed LMI energy efficiency EAM.

Program Costs 
National Grid is requesting recovery for hiring 3 new FTEs to support the program. 
Increase in FTEs: The Clean Energy Advisors aim to discuss with LMI customers what clean 
energy program opportunities are available to them and which programs may be most beneficial 
to them, help them with filling out program applications and scheduling services, and connect the 
various different clean energy programs and sources of utility, local, state and federal funding to 
help meet as much of the customers’ needs as possible.

•

Program Title: LMI Marketing and Outreach 
Brief Description: 
The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid NY (“KEDNY”) and KeySpan Gas East 
Corporation d/b/a National Grid (“KEDLI”) (collectively, the “Companies”) are requesting $500 
thousand to develop and implement marketing and outreach aimed at increasing participation in 
the Home Energy Assistance Program (“HEAP”), the Energy Affordability Program (“EAP”) and 
other bill assistance programs, promoting self-certification in EAP through other eligible social 
service programs, and providing a holistic and coordinated marketing experience by creating a link 
between low-to-moderate (“LMI”) income assistance programs and LMI EE programs.  
In addition to the proposed marketing and outreach budget, the program costs include 1 full-time 
equivalent (“FTE”) to design, develop and oversee the marketing and outreach. 
Program Justification: 
 LMI customers are still largely unaware of the programs and services available to assist them with 
their energy usage and energy bills. A survey conducted by the Companies revealed that financial 
assistance is at the top of mind for customers and 60 percent of customers are interested or very 
interested in learning about new programs and other assistance programs. Investing in marketing 
and outreach to LMI customers will help bridge this gap and give them the knowledge and 
understanding to seek the help that they need. Additionally, as the Companies continue to attend 
more in-person events and seek new avenues to be present in the communities they serve, funding 
is needed to attend events and provide materials to customers. Currently, LMI customers receive 
communications from the Companies for a variety of different reasons and streamlining the 
messages as well as tailoring them to smaller groups of customers will provide a more personalized 
approach. KEDNY and KEDLI customer bases differ greatly in demographics and lifestyle so it 
will be important to design marketing and outreach that addresses customers’ distinct needs in 
each territory and reflects their characteristics.  
The marketing and outreach program will support New York’s Energy Affordability Policy 
Proceeding goal of keeping household energy burdens at or below 6% by informing customers of 
programs, services and tools to better manage their energy usage and reduce their energy bill. 
These resources will also help to enroll more customers and support the newly proposed EAP 
EAM.  
Program Costs 
National Grid is requesting recovery for an LMI marketing and outreach program aimed at 
increasing customer awareness and understanding of the programs and services available to 
income eligible customers to help them better manage their energy bills as well as to hiring 1 new 
FTEs to support the program.   
Program Budget: The proposed budget will cover outreach and materials including bill inserts, 
brochures and flyers, direct mail, web and digital media, webinars, and event materials. It will also

allow the Companies to test new channels and approaches to outreach and communication with its 
customers.  
Increase in FTEs: The Marketing an Outreach Specialist will be responsible for designing, 
developing and implementing the LMI marketing and outreach program to improve awareness of 
assistance opportunities for LMI customers.

Program Title: Collection Residential Account Management 
Brief Description: 
The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid NY (“KEDNY”) and KeySpan Gas East 
Corporation d/b/a National Grid (“KEDLI”) (collectively, the “Companies”) suspended collection 
activity in March 2020 due to the impacts from COVID throughout our service territory.  
Customer protections were extended to customers through the Parker Mosley (Chapters 108 and 
126 of the Laws of 2020) legislation passed in 2020 and the Parker Richardson legislation passed 
in 2021. During this period, residential customers had the option to attest to financial hardship 
and various customer protections including protection from disconnection for non-payment.  
Residential field disconnections slowly resumed in June 2022 at KEDNY and July 2022 at 
KEDLI.  During the disconnection moratorium both residential customers built up large arrears 
balances. Despite several Statewide programs including the Residential Arrears Supplement 
(RAS) program, Emergency Rental Assistance Program (ERAP) and Phases 1 and 2 of New 
York Bill Relief providing arrears relief to the residential customer base both the number of 
customers and arrears balance have grown since during this period.  As of February 2023, the 
number of customers in 60-day arrears increased by 32% at KEDNY and 30% at KEDLI 
compared to February 2020 and accounts receivable balances increased by 59% at KEDNY and 
86% at KEDLI.   
To help address the increase in arrears, the Companies propose to increase staffing levels for our 
Collection Residential Account Management (CRAM) program. This program specializes in direct 
engagement through phone calls and email with high balance residential customers that require 
extra assistance in reducing their account balance and preventing service termination.  The 
program focuses on building relationships with these customers to provide financial options and 
assistance, improve the customer experience and to help avoid service termination.  
Program Justification: 
The additional staff will help manage additional customers through the program. Low income and 
high balance customers receive the most benefit from this program as the customer is provided a 
single point of contact to help them manage their arrears, provide guidance on payment agreement 
options that will address their situation and potential assistance programs that may be available to 
them. This program helps customer avoid service terminations by working directly with them to 
reduce their past due balance.   
During the winter of 2021/22, the Collections team piloted an expansion of the existing program. 
A total of 803 overtime hours were worked by the team working with customers who had balances 
of $1,000 - $5,000.  The team collected $332k at an estimated cost of $27k. This equates to a 
collection rate of over $12 for every dollar spent.

Program Title: Permit Third Party Vendors To Use OUT OF STATE Agents 
Brief Description: 
It has become increasingly common for call center agents to work remotely. As a result, the 
Companies, as well as our outsourced vendor partners, are leveraging work from home options in 
order to attract and retain quality employees. In doing so, it has also become clear that recruiting 
for such positions in the Northeast, specifically downstate NY is particularly challenging. 
Retention also continues to be of concern due to increased wages in NY. 
We are proposing to allow our third party vendors to use agents located outside New York State. 
Enabling vendors to flex staff across their teams will allow them to minimize price increases and 
attract candidates from a wider range of employment markets.  These vendor employees would be 
located outside of New York State, but within the continental US.  National Fuel, Central Hudson, 
RG&E, and NYSEG currently use out of state call enter representatives.   
Program Justification: 
Turnover rates are running at ~10%, increase of 50% compared to pre-Covid. Some driving factors 
to attrition: 
•
Job availability: many opportunities available in the job market, in 2022, workers were
more comfortable to leave their current role for one that they feel is better suited for them
(i.e. higher pay, remote work, flexible schedule, benefits, etc.)
•
Increasing pay rates: companies in 2022 and year to date, have been increasing their
starting pay rates to attract more candidates-this puts more pressure on employers retaining
their current workforce
Managing attrition rates to create a more consistent tenured agent population which will drive: 
a.
improved first call resolution
b. customer satisfaction
c.
employee satisfaction
d. reduced training costs
Program Costs 
There are no costs to achieve.  
Customer Benefits: 
Customers who benefit from this approach will result in: 
•
Improved first call resolution
•
Customer satisfaction

Program Title: Utilize Near Off-Shore Spanish Call Centers 
Brief Description: 
The Companies currently use translation services and the process is cumbersome.  Although the 
Companies recruit and attempt to staff bi-lingual agents, they have not been able to successfully 
maintain sufficient staff to provide full in-language support for customers even though the wage 
structure is higher for a bi-lingual agent.  Recruiting for such positions in the Northeast, 
specifically downstate NY, is particularly challenging. Retention also continues to be of concern 
due to increased wages in NY. 
The Companies propose to provide our vendor partners the ability to utilize near off-shore Spanish 
speaking call centers to provide in-language services to our DNY customers.  
Program Justification: 
Provide seamless Spanish in-language experience for a significant number of customers resulting 
in: 
a) improved first call resolution
b) customer satisfaction
c) employee satisfaction
d) reduced training costs
Program Costs 
There are no costs to achieve.  
Customer Benefits: 
Customers who benefit from this approach will result in: 
•
Improved first call resolution
•
Customer satisfaction
•
Reduction in time spent handling their inquiry
•
Recent testimonials below provided from NE customers where this approach has already
been implemented:
Customer A on January 11, 2023 said:  "Me siento bendecida por tu atencion con cariño, forma de 
ser y en español"  
Translation: “I feel blessed by your attention with affection, way of being and in Spanish” 
Customer B on January 20, 2023 said: "Gracias por atenderme en español, cuidese Dios me lo 
bendiga" 
Translation: “Thank you for assisting me in Spanish, take care God bless you” 
Customer C on January 23, 2023 said: "Muy felices porque ustedes hablan español, muchas 
gracias" 
Translation: “I’m very happy because you speak Spanish. Thank you very much!”

Program Title: Customer Experience 
Brief Description: 
The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid NY (“KEDNY”) and KeySpan Gas East 
Corporation d/b/a National Grid (“KEDLI”) (collectively, the “Companies”) are focused on 
enhancing each customer’s experience when interacting with the Companies. The Companies are 
responding to the ever-changing business demands that have become evident over the past few 
years, which has increasingly focused on digital and process transformation to maintain high levels 
of customer satisfaction and provide the ability to deliver the level of service that customers expect. 
Program Justification: 
The primary driver of these efforts is to improve the reliability of critical systems, enhance existing 
business process, as well as overall customer experience.  Dependence on traditional customer 
interactions via telephone and mail is inefficient in today’s world when compared to the newly 
proposed digital focused Customer Experience.  The Companies’ currently utilize technology that 
relies on manual processes. The proposed Customer Experience enables technology-based 
solutions that customers are seeking. 
Key focus areas include: 
1) Optimizing Contact Center operations through management of internal and vendor
workforce planning allows for a lower response time to customer calls.  The Companies
will also enable vendors to handle the full context of all call types, including frequent
questions, ensuring that customers are informed of value-added products, and optimizing
call distribution based upon cost and operational performance.
2) Shifting to Digital Adoption:
a.
Improved Customer experiences on the Unified Web Portal to manage billing
accounts with single email sign on
b. Proactively provide the right information via customers’ preferred communication
channel (via email or text)
c.
Provide customers with simpler, effortless and personalized experiences through
improving voice self-service (interactive voice response)
d. Delivering value through improved and new experiences on digital channels,
leveraging virtual assistants (digital assistant)
e.
Delivering an enhanced digital platform (My Business Account) for Commercial
and Industrial Customers by streamlining multi account management
3) Focus on process enhancements and performance management to drive efficient and
effective calls.  By optimizing the greet and authenticate process, customers will not have
to provide duplicate information.
4) Successfully addressing an inquiry during the customers’ first contact with the call center,
preventing a call back with the same issue (First Call Resolution).  Areas of opportunity
include Customer Moves and Billing interactions that make up 65.8 % of repeat calls today.


--- Page 181 ---
Exhibit ___ (CP-2) 
Schedule 3 
Page 2 of 2 
Related is an effort to reduce call transfer rates, aiming to keep calls contained with one 
Customer Service Representative. 
5) Create operational efficiencies through the deployment of Robotic Process Automation
(RPA) to reduce manual efforts. Reducing exception volumes and automating processes
will increase standardization and creates capacity for representatives to help customers
with more complex or value add tasks.
6) The Billing and Collections office will use a centralized platform to optimize workforce
performance with the ability to forecast, plan and schedule more effectively.   The
Companies’ will be able to more accurately measure employee performance (such as
shrinkage and work handle time) and improve productivity
7) Customer Service Representatives will encourage customer participation in the paperless
program for an easier and more convenient billing experience.     The Companies will
measure and remediate key drivers of e-bill disenrollment and redesign email notifications
to increase efficacy and ease of use. Customer analytics will target customer segments and
tailor marketing campaigns to promote electronic billing accordingly.
Program Costs 
IT exhibits and individual revenue requirements are being submitted as part of the Rate Case 
Testimony. 
Customer Benefits: 
Customers will have a better management of internal and vendor workforce planning by allowing 
for lower response time to customer calls and more proactive customer notifications. Customers 
will also have more opportunities to utilize the Companies self-service and digital channels to 
resolve additional transactions.  As a result, the Companies are eliminating bottlenecks and 
inefficiencies driving customer authentication and transfer within and between centers, resulting 
in quicker resolution of customer inquiries.  Additionally, extending e-bill adoption across our 
customer base will enable an easier and more convenient billing experience.  
Creating digital capabilities will allow customers to self- serve inquiries in the manner they prefer 
(chat bot, web, interactive voice response) and decrease time spent on phone. Process Automation 
will focus on decreasing manual, repetitive tasks (for agents) which increases time to focus on 
more complex customer issues that result in an improved customer experience. This provides the 
customer with consistency and high satisfaction. Customer agents will educate customers on the 
ease of accessing their billing information and paying their bills electronically.  Finally, by 
delivering outbound communications via email and text messaging, customers will have correct 
information in real time. For example, communications about outages or upcoming storms.  
Savings associated with these programs are part of the Evolution savings that are reflected in the 
Companies’ proposed revenue requirements.

Exhibit ___ (CP-5) 
Supplemental Information: 
No-fee Model for Debit and Credit Card Payment Proposal 


--- Page 207 ---
Exhibit ___ (CP-5) 
Page 1 of 2 
Program Title: Fee Free Card Payments (Residential Customers Only) 
Brief Description: 
The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid NY (“KEDNY”) and KeySpan Gas East 
Corporation d/b/a National Grid (“KEDLI”) (collectively, the “Companies”) offer residential 
customers the ability to pay their utility bill with a credit/debit card via a third-party card processor 
Speedpay (ACI Worldwide).  Customers can pay their utility bills via CC/DC in several ways. 
First, customers can pay using a card on the National Grid website or through the National Grid 
mobile app.  Second, customers enrolled in electronic billing receive an email with a link that takes 
them to a site where they can pay via CC/DC.  Finally, customers can pay by phone using the 
automated telephone system (“IVR”).  Residential customers who use one of these options to make 
a CC/DC payment incur a convenience fee of $1.75 for payment amounts up to $1,300.00, and 
they can make 5 payments in a 30-day period. National Grid does not currently accept recurring 
CC/DC payments because customers must actively accept the vendor’s transaction fee at the time 
of each transaction. 
The Companies propose to include in base rates the estimated cost to recover the fees incurred by 
KEDNY and KEDLI when residential customers make CC/DC payments. This is referred to as a 
“no-fee model.”  The no-fee model would eliminate the per-transaction cost to customers paying 
by CC/DC by shifting that cost into base rates.  
Program Justification: 
Shift in Payment Methods:  The Companies continue to see an increase in customers using 
electronic forms of payment with non-electronic forms of payment such as check decreasing. 
KEDNY saw its check volumes drop 11.5% and KEDLI 14% compared to overall payment 
methods from 2017 to 2022.  Customer have shifted to electronic forms of payment with CC/DC 
seeing a 3.3% increase in overall payment volumes for KEDNY and KEDLI saw a 2.4% increase 
in that same period.  To put this into perspective, in 2022 checks made up 15% of KEDNY and 
21% of KEDLI for overall payment methods, while cards made up 7% for KEDNY and 5% in 
KEDLI.   
Increase Customer Satisfaction:  In 2021 John Hazen, managing director of utilities at market 
research company J.D. Power, stated in an article on creditcards.com, “customer satisfaction 
ratings for utilities that assess those convenience fees are lower than ratings for utilities that fold 
those convenience fees into their rates. Transitioning to a no-fee model will benefit customers LMI 
customers who receive public assistance benefits via pre-paid debit cards. Under the current model, 
these customers can pay their utility bill with their pre-paid debit card but must use a portion of 
the benefits to cover the vendor fee for CC/DC payments, resulting in an added economic 
disadvantage. Adopting a no-fee model will eliminate the need for a portion of public assistance 
benefits being used to pay this administrative fee.  The National Association of State Utility 
Consumer Advocates noted in a study (nasuca.org) argued that convenience fees unfairly punish 
utility customers who might make several payments in a month. Also, with a no-fee model the 
Companies can apply with VISA for a utility rate that would potentially lower the fees even lower 


--- Page 208 ---
Exhibit ___ (CP-5) 
Page 2 of 2 
than current rates.  Additionally, in customer feedback they have indicated that the ability to make 
CC/DC payments with no fee would improve the customer experience. Customers have become 
accustomed to paying without convenience fees when shopping online or paying for services like 
their mobile phone.  Online companies for example can insert these fees into their overall product 
costs/pricing. Moving to a no-fee model will help meet customer expectations for improved 
payment options and enhance customer ease and overall experience. 
Program Costs 
National Grid is requesting recovery for these card fees for residential customers. 
Program Budget: The proposed budget will cover the convenience fee that customers currently 
pay when using a credit/debit card via Speedpay (ACI Worldwide).  
The Companies forecast the following program costs:

Schedule 1 
Weatherization Health & Safety 


--- Page 211 ---
Exhibit ___ (CP-6) 
Schedule 1 
Page 1 of 3 
Program Title: Weatherization Health & Safety 
Brief Description: 
In 2022, The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid NY (“KEDNY”) and KeySpan 
Gas East Corporation d/b/a National Grid (“KEDLI”) (collectively, the “Companies”) provided 
Low-and-Moderate Income (“LMI”) and Disadvantaged Communities customers remediation 
services to eliminate health and safety barriers toward participating in weatherization programs. 
These activities have included, but are not limited to, remediation of carbon monoxide hazards, 
mold, pests, insufficient wiring or ventilation, plumbing problems, blocked access to spaces in the 
home, unsafe appliances, and unsafe condition of the customer-owned gas line.  Upon completion, 
the actual weatherization is completed.   
These efforts were initially a shareholder-funded venture.  Due to the successful uptick in 
participation by LMI customers, the Companies propose to continue the offerings through the rate 
year and beyond and to include funding for the remediation program in their revenue requirements. 
Examples of the remediation program are as follows: 
Project 1: Customer received a Home Energy Assessment through the KEDLI HEAT program 
where they installed a carbon monoxide detector and smoke detector.  They suspected that the attic 
had vermiculite and left behind a health and safety form.  The customer was referred to the 
Weatherization Health and Safety program.  The program removed the vermiculite through 
cooperation of several contractors that provided testing and monitoring during abatement, 
remediation and proper ventilation.  Once removed, weatherization measures were able to be 
performed such as air sealing attic flat areas, 16” of blown in attic insulation, insulating wrap to 
curtain walls, installing baffles and insulating attic hatches.  Costs: $32,093.88 for health and 
safety work, $12,734.49 for weatherization work 
Project 2: Customer received a Home Energy Assessment through KEDLI HEAT where they 
installed 2 carbon monoxide detectors and installed and weather-stripped the ceiling hatch, 
installed 3 smoke detectors and one temperature and pressure drip tube on the direct hot water 
tank.  It was recommended that the boiler be replaced as there was high carbon monoxide in the 
flue.  The assessment also found that there were some electrical issues as well as a leak in the roof. 
Through the cooperation of several contractors, the roof was replaced, the unsafe wiring was 
addressed and the bathroom was re-energized so proper ventilation could be provided.  The 
contractor covered the service panel and junction into the attic as well.  Once this work was 
complete, the boiler was able to be replaced and weatherization measures were provided such as 
16” blown in attic insulation, attic air sealing, installing baffles and spraying the rim joists.  Costs: 
$22,025 for health and safety work, $19,736.13 for weatherization and boiler replacement. 


--- Page 212 ---
Exhibit ___ (CP-6) 
Schedule 1 
Page 2 of 3 
Program Justification: 
Make Weatherization Accessible to All. Over seven hundred LMI and Disadvantaged Community 
customers took advantage of this supplemental program, over the course of the last rate plan.  
Increase Disadvantaged Community Participation. The Climate Leadership Council Protection 
Act (“CLCPA”) designates that at least 35% of the benefits of clean energy be directed to 
Disadvantaged Communities. And, while the Companies aim to exceed these expectations, 
sometimes external drivers like the examples stated above prevent these efforts. Initiatives like the 
Weatherization Health & Safety Program are imperative in ensuring that everyone has equal access 
to more complex programs that deliver longer term energy savings. 
Meet Energy Efficiency Targets. Increased access for all eligible customers ensures the 
Companies’ ability to support the greenhouse gas (“GHG”) emission reduction goals set forth in 
New York’s CLCPA.   
Program Costs 
National Grid is requesting recovery for outsourced remediation activities. The Companies’ work 
with their implementation contractor as well as NYSERDA to   partner with the necessary 
installation and repair contractors to complete the projects. The Companies are then invoiced upon 
completion. 
Incentives: The proposed budget will cover all required services to prepare a site for 
weatherization. This may include ventilation fans, installation of carbon monoxide detectors, 
tuning natural gas equipment, septic and plumbing troubleshooting, roof replacement, removal of 
mold and asbestos, pest control, moisture control, and correcting potential electrical hazards. 
Administration: The proposed budget will cover administrative fees associated with working with 
the Companies’ implementation contractor who is responsible for managing the contractor 
network, incentive payment, quality control, contact center services, reporting and other 
administrative work.  
Marketing: The proposed budget will cover minimal costs associated with marketing the programs 
to make LMI customers aware that this opportunity is available to them.  
Home Assessments: The proposed budget will cover an additional more in-depth Health and Safety 
Assessment as needed to identify and scope out the existing health and/or safety issues in the home. 


--- Page 213 ---
Exhibit ___ (CP-6) 
Schedule 1 
Page 3 of 3 
The Companies forecast the following program costs: 
Customer Benefits: 
Customers who participate in the Weatherization Health & Safety program will benefit from: 

Opportunity to receive weatherization services which can result in reduced energy costs.

Obtaining a no-direct-cost home safety upgrade.

Guided program delivery.


Program Title: Disadvantaged Community Outreach 
Brief Description: 
The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid NY (“KEDNY”) and KeySpan Gas East 
Corporation d/b/a National Grid (“KEDLI”) (collectively, the “Companies”) will test multiple 
strategies to engage customers within Disadvantaged Communities to participate in Energy 
Efficiency programs. This effort will include in-person events, localized social media campaigns, 
distribution of innovative education materials, community partnerships, and customer referrals. 
The goal here is to identify key barriers to engagement in EE programs within these communities 
and the best strategies to overcome them. 
The Companies are committed to exceeding the Climate Leadership Council Protection Act 
(“CLCPA”) goal of directing 35% of energy efficiency resources to Disadvantaged Communities. 
These research techniques and strategies would align directly with enrolling customers in the 
Companies’ programs during the rate plan. The program also aims to identify normative beliefs in 
these communities, related to energy efficiency programs, and to provide active education as 
needed. 
Program Justification: 
Increase Disadvantaged Community Participation. Customized education and communication 
strategies will enable clear messaging to this target audience, delivering an uptick in program 
participation, as well as an understanding of all offerings. The outcomes of this effort will ensure 
future outreach and engagement utilizes the most effective channels within each community. 
Meet Energy Efficiency Targets. Increased program participation, in addition to improved 
community trust, will assist the Companies in delivering more complex and long-term transition 
programs, where the value propositions may be hard to understand or overshadowed by other 
barriers and affordability issues. 
Program Costs 
National Grid is requesting recovery for targeted outreach campaigns and communication 
materials, including community events, mailers, brochures, media campaigns, and customer 
referrals.  
Community Events & Partnerships: The proposed budget will cover initial setup and materials to 
host localized in-person educational events. 
Delivery of Innovative Materials: This includes marketing development, print and distribution 
costs, social media campaigns, as well as educational mailers and brochures. 
Customer Referral Incentives: Scope and design of a referral process, incentives for referral, and 
customer acceptance testing.
